0,2026-09-19,"How much data does a phone secretly send to Google when you are not using it? Our team measured 72 continuous hours of network packets from an idle Android phone sitting untouched in our lab. Here is our raw dataset, our test setup, and what it means for your privacy.
Imagine placing your smartphone face-down on your nightstand before going to sleep. You assume that because the screen is dark and you haven’t opened an app in hours, the phone is sitting quietly doing nothing.
Our data proves the exact opposite: a standard factory Android phone never rests. In our tests, an idle phone woke up an average of 348 times every single hour—nearly 6 times every minute—to whisper your Wi-Fi details, device serials, and background telemetry back to Alphabet data centers. Over a 24-hour day, that adds up to over 8,300 background transmissions without you ever tapping the screen.
Check off the Google services you have replaced in your daily life. Our tool calculates your freedom score and estimates how many of the 8,920 daily telemetry pings you have eliminated.
You are currently relying on default Google tools. Your devices broadcast an estimated 8,920 background telemetry pings to Alphabet servers every 24 hours.
Tested Replacements: Brave Search, DuckDuckGo, Kagi
Cuts personal search query profiling, ad intent tracking, and AI search harvesting.
Tested Replacements: Brave, Firefox, LibreWolf, Mullvad
Removes Chrome's Privacy Sandbox ad tracking, browser fingerprinting, and account sync snooping.
Tested Replacements: Proton Mail, Tuta Mail, Fastmail
Prevents automated parsing of flight receipts, online purchases, tax notices, and password reset links.
Tested Replacements: Proton Drive, CryptPad, Nextcloud
Guarantees zero-knowledge client-side encryption so tech platforms cannot scan your personal documents.
Tested Replacements: Immich, Ente Photos, Local SSDs
Stops cloud facial recognition, GPS heatmaps of your daily routines, and photo EXIF metadata mining.
Tested Replacements: Obsidian, Notesnook, Proton Calendar
Keeps private journal entries, doctor appointments, and daily family schedules completely encrypted.
Tested Replacements: Organic Maps, CoMaps, Magic Earth, OSM
Halts 24/7 background location logging, Wi-Fi router beacon scans, and frequent-location history.
Tested Replacements: GrapheneOS, CalyxOS, Pure AOSP
The ultimate privacy step. Cuts 99.4% of all idle background Alphabet socket connections (from 348/hr to 0.0/hr).
Includes exact timestamps, device builds, destination IPs, ASN classifications, port numbers, and byte payloads.
When testing phone tracking, many reviewers make the mistake of installing a software app on the phone to monitor it. But if the operating system is sending telemetry, it can easily hide or bypass monitoring apps.
Our team did something different: we built an external hardware wiretap. Every electrical radio wave leaving the phone's antenna was captured by an independent firewall computer before it ever touched the internet.
We purchased three brand new Google Pixel 8 smartphones (128GB, factory unlocked). We tested them side-by-side on our workbench.
We created a private, closed Wi-Fi network (WPA3-Enterprise) that had zero other laptops, TVs, or printers connected. Only the test phones were on this network.
The Wi-Fi router was plugged directly into an enterprise pfSense firewall running Wireshark packet capture software. Every packet was mirrored and logged with nanosecond accuracy.
All phones were plugged into wall power, locked, and left undisturbed for 72 straight hours (259,200 seconds) without any human interaction.
We analyzed the packet headers and destination IP addresses across Alphabet's Autonomous System Number (ASN 15169). Here is what the phone was secretly uploading while locked:
Even if you turn GPS location off, Google Play Services listens for the unique MAC addresses (BSSIDs) of every Wi-Fi router in your apartment building or office and uploads them to checkin.gstatic.com. Google cross-references these signals to pinpoint your physical location within meters.
The phone periodically transmits carrier configuration profiles, SIM card serial hashes, and unique Android hardware fingerprints to device-provisioning.googleapis.com, tying your physical handset directly to your Google profile.
To make push notifications feel instant, Android maintains a persistent open socket to mtalk.google.com:5228. Every few minutes, a heartbeat packet confirms to Google what Wi-Fi network and IP address your device is currently connected to.
Google Photos reaches out to photosdata-pa.googleapis.com to verify sync tokens, prefetch facial cluster tags, and update album states in the background.
The home screen search bar polls suggestqueries.google.com to pre-download trending keywords and speech recognition language models before you ever tap search.
The table below isolates outbound socket requests initiated by individual pre-installed Google ecosystem applications over a 24-hour window with zero user interaction:
If you open degoogle-telemetry-2026.csv in Microsoft Excel, Apple Numbers, Google Sheets, or Python, here is what every single column represents:
Use our service breakdown table above to understand where your personal data leaks the most. You don't have to change everything overnight—replacing just Search and Browser stops 40% of advertising profiling in 5 minutes.
You can copy the destination domains (play.googleapis.com, checkin.gstatic.com, telemetry.google.com) directly into your Pi-hole, AdGuard Home, or NextDNS custom blocklists to shield every device on your home Wi-Fi.
This dataset is released under Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 (CC BY 4.0). You are encouraged to download the raw CSV, build your own charts, analyze packet timing distributions, and quote our findings with attribution.

2,2026-09-19,"If you’ve used MicroPython much, you know that debugging usually amounts to printing a few things out, trying your code out, and then repeating. But [ghi-electronics] wants you to have full on-chip source-code debugging in Visual Studio. You don’t need anything special to use it — just a supported MicroPython host and the same USB cable you program with now.
The downside is that you either have to build a custom MicroPython image and flash it or use one that they include. They support several Raspberry Pi Pico versions and ESP32 chips, as well. However, at least one “odd” Pico we had lying around wouldn’t take the firmware. A stock one did, and it worked as you would expect. A little more investigation showed the odd Pico (an RP2040 GEEK) probably did take the firmware; it just produced an error during setup. There is a known problem with Linux and the ESP32-S3 having similar behavior.

Of course, you can build your own image, but now you are talking a bit more work to get a toolchain and all the dependencies together. For simple programs, you might not need a full-blown debugger. But it is nice to be able to peek at variables and see the control flow visually.
There are a few limitations documented on GitHub. For example, you can’t catch exceptions that the code already catches, although, presumably, you could set a breakpoint in the exception handler. Breakpoints halt all threads. There are a few other limits, but nothing we’d consider a showstopper.
Full Python has some debugging assistance built into it. MicroPython has some of the same things, but it isn’t trivial to build a debugger.

This is amazong finally. This will save so much time. thonny is so slow when debugging and having to write so many prints to identify the problems was taking up so much time
All the blog noise about it made me want to try it for a small project a while ago. It’s the worst kind of pain imaginable. Literally the worst of all worlds concentrated in a tight spot, with zero benefits. And yet, somehow it’s a thing.
(It was a comment re: “micropython is a joke”, which magically disappeared, not about the debugger from the article)
I’ve used micropython quite a bit. But it’s not for every project. It works well if you just need to read out some pins/sensors and send that through USB/network/bluetooth as a one-off.
If you have a more complex project, the benefit of quickly hacking something together doesn’t weight against the speed loss and lack of compile time checks. So having a better debugger option would help a bit with more complex projects. But I personally would still use C++ for those.
We might point out that there is a difference between Visual Studio and Visual Studio Code.
The last one is a totally ugly piece of crap.
I used micropython quite a bit and for small quick projects it is fine. I discovere after the program exceeds 80kb cannot run on pico due to memory issues. At first i started using mpy-cross as a work around, then I realized compiling from C wpuld be even quicker so switched to C sdk and loving it.

3,2026-09-19,"The cash burn figure everyone is quoting moved the other way, improving from about $305bn in May to $278bn in July. Free cash flow does not capture what partners are carrying.
The Financial Times reported that OpenAI expects negative free cash flow of $278bn between 2026 and 2030, from a July presentation prepared for a computing deal. That figure is an improvement on the roughly $305bn the company projected in May, while the compute and infrastructure line has risen to about $856bn against the roughly $600bn target it gave investors publicly in February. Spending can rise while burn falls because much of the build is financed by partners rather than by OpenAI.
The Financial Times reported that OpenAI expects negative free cash flow of $278bn between 2026 and the end of 2030, citing a company presentation prepared in July for a computing deal. Samantha Oltman wrote up the figures for Bloomberg, which corrected its story shortly after publication.
The same materials put revenue at $350bn in 2030, against roughly $36bn this year, and compute and infrastructure at about $856bn across the period. Three numbers, and the smallest one is getting the headlines.
An earlier projection in May put negative free cash flow for the same period at roughly $305bn. The July version is $278bn.
So the number being reported as alarming is an improvement of about $27bn on OpenAI’s own previous estimate. That does not make it small, and it does make the framing odd.
In February the company publicly reset expectations. It told investors its compute target was around $600bn by 2030, a figure presented at the time as a moderation.
Five months later a private presentation carries $856bn. That is roughly 43% higher than the number given publicly in February.
One caveat belongs here. The February figure was described as compute and the July figure as computing power and infrastructure, so the categories may not be identical, and part of the gap could be definitional rather than real.
Spending rises, burn falls. That combination is possible when the spending does not sit on your own balance sheet.
OpenAI does not hold an investment-grade credit rating, which is why its financing runs through other people. Nvidia has been in talks to guarantee $250bn of data centre debt, letting lenders price against the chipmaker’s credit instead.
The pattern repeats across the build. Oracle is spending more on data centres than it earns in a quarter, much of it against OpenAI commitments, and the capital expenditure lands on Oracle’s accounts rather than OpenAI’s.
Structure matters as much as scale. SB Energy took $5.5bn in OpenAI warrants to sign a 20-year lease, which converts a capital commitment into an operating one and pays for it in equity rather than cash.
Free cash flow is a measure of money leaving a specific entity. It is not a measure of obligations created, and the two diverge sharply when a build is financed by vendors, landlords and partners.
That is why $856bn of compute can coexist with $278bn of burn. The difference is being carried by somebody.
The credit market has already shown where the strain is. Oracle needed PIMCO to anchor $10bn of a $16.3bn data centre financing after US banks stepped back.
When banks retreat from a name like Oracle, the terms available further down the chain are worse. The guarantees and warrants are not elegance, they are what the market required.
Revenue rising from about $36bn to $350bn by 2030 is close to a tenfold increase in four years. Every other figure in the presentation is downstream of it.
The burn is not an independent forecast. It is what remains after the revenue assumption is subtracted from the spending assumption, so a revenue miss does not shave the burn, it compounds it.
Reporting the $278bn as the headline risk inverts the logic. The spending is largely contracted, and the revenue is the part that has to show up.
OpenAI raised $122bn in March at an $852bn valuation, and the FT reports it is on track to exhaust that by 2028. The projection period runs two years beyond the money.
That is the context for the listing timetable and for talks the FT describes as valuing the company at about $1.2 trillion. A company that needs capital before 2028 has a reason to be in the market before then.
These are projections in a document prepared to win a computing deal, not audited accounts, and they have already been revised twice this year. OpenAI has also paused its Stargate site in the UK over energy costs and copyright rules.
Projects get delayed and targets get rewritten, which is an argument for treating any single figure with caution. It applies to $856bn as much as to $278bn.
Watch whether the $856bn figure appears anywhere OpenAI can be held to it. A number in a deal presentation and a number in a listing prospectus carry different consequences.
Watch the guarantees. If partner balance sheets are carrying the difference between the spending and the burn, the exposure worth tracking is theirs.

4,2026-09-19,"Some officials and state media have long openly described the industry as a ‘mafia’ system that transfers costs to households.
Save
Share
Tehran, Iran – Hossein, a 32-year-old marketing specialist based in Tehran, has been thinking of replacing his 13-year-old Iranian-made car with a newer model.
Even though he earns about four-and-a-half times the minimum wage after a recent pay rise – his salary is now close to 900 million rials (about $390 at the current exchange rate) – imported vehicles are not even remotely affordable for him.
Even finding a locally made car might be out of his budget, with an economic crisis gripping the country since the United States and Israel launched a surprise war on Iran on February 28 and later enacted a crippling siege and sanctions on the country.
“I’m losing hope of ever being able to buy a new domestic production car too, unless the country opens up and becomes a bit more normal again,” Hossein, who asked to keep his full name confidential for security reasons, told Al Jazeera.
His old manual Peugeot 206 model, an originally French-made car but now produced domestically after foreign counterparts left Iran due to sanctions, can fetch up to 10 billion rials ($4,350) if he sells it towards making a new purchase. However, his replacement options are limited.
Upgrading to a slightly improved Peugeot 207 with an automatic gearbox could cost him 28 billion rials ($12,170) now. This means that, after selling his car, he would need more than 20 months of his entire salary.
A domestic sedan Shahin model costs more than 31 billion rials ($13,480), and a crossover Reera is priced at more than 43 billion rials ($18,700).
With these options, he would need to save about 24 months and 37 months of his whole salary, respectively, and that is if prices remain stable and he does not spend a rial on anything else. But the reality of the fast-rising living costs and lagging incomes in Iran means he can barely put aside any money, let alone afford a new car.
 

 
Domestic car prices have mostly risen 40 to 80 percent since the start of the war, while some vehicles sell for more than 130 percent of their September 2025 costs.
The costs of maintaining the vehicles are also rising much faster than people’s salaries. Domestically produced tyres, motor oil, brake pads and clutch kits have at least doubled since last year, with some car parts having more than tripled in price.
Domestic vehicles have generally low safety standards, meaning that they contribute to staggering road accident deaths. At least 1,609 Iranians have been killed so far on intercity roads in the current month of Shahrivar of the solar Hijri calendar, which ends on September 22. More than 20,000 people lose their lives on the roads every year. In comparison, fewer people in the whole of the European Union, nearly five times Iran’s population, died on roads last year.
Iran’s fuel-guzzling cars also contribute to smoke-congested city streets, degrade vehicles faster and increase fuel costs, just as petrol prices have risen for users.
Experts say, due to a combination of protected state-linked businesses, privileged access, economic isolation and a curtailing of imports, Iranian households have no choice but to pay exorbitant prices – compared with their salaries – to buy low-quality cars.
The damage from the war, including the extensive bombing of multiple steel giants by Israel and the US, has only added insult to injury. The naval blockade of Iran’s southern ports has prevented goods coming in from popular neighbouring markets like the United Arab Emirates.
Under such circumstances, domestic vehicle manufacturers have little incentive to improve.
End customers are also beset by government charges, currency and financing costs, margins levied by murky intermediaries – and in numerous documented cases, industry corruption.
“People are forced to buy expensive low-quality cars whose real prices should be a quarter of global prices, and this is a direct harm done to them,” Mohammad Rashidi, a member of the presiding board of Iran’s parliament, told local media on Saturday. “The traces of a mafia system are visible throughout the process.”
His claims echo those of other officials and state-linked media, who have openly described the industry as resembling an organised crime operation.
According to the latest figures released by state media, about 233,000 cars were manufactured or assembled in Iran in the first five months of 2026, compared with 366,000 the year before. Only about 25,000 vehicles were imported in that period.
Only a handful of state-linked companies or intermediaries are allowed to import vehicles, with duties plus value-added tax increasing final prices up to 200 percent.
The government and parliament have discussed lowering import tariffs this year, with no agreement announced so far.
The premiums are more visible for high-end cars, with a 2026 Toyota Land Cruiser VXR going for approximately 660 billion rials ($287,000) in Iran at the moment, while the same model is available for about $86,000 in the UAE. The same story applies to most other models, at different rates depending on the rarity of the vehicle and the availability of parts.
The price of a mid-range Chinese-designed SUV sold as Exeed VX in international markets is listed at about $32,000 in China, while it is priced at about $42,000 in the UAE. The same car, assembled from imported parts by a state-linked company in Iran under a different name, currently costs Iranian customers the equivalent of $53,000.
The bizarre situation was on full display during a three-day “international” car exhibition in Tehran last week that mostly featured Chinese-manufactured vehicles. These remain available in Iran despite the US sanctions, since Tehran exports almost all of its oil to China and barters this for goods, including cars.
Some domestic manufacturers and importers were absent, either because they had no products to offer or because they had angry customers who registered months ago to get vehicles they never received. Spare parts for some of the vehicles on display are currently either not available in the Iranian market or cost several times their price in international markets.
Even the cheapest vehicles offered at the exhibition were completely unaffordable to the average Iranian. It would take a worker on the minimum wage with standard allowances 50 years to buy an XPENG G9, a Chinese-made electric SUV priced at 120 billion rials ($52,150), on his or her salary, without spending a rial on food, housing or clothing.
Still, there were huge queues outside the exhibition centre each of the three days.
“It was sad because most people just came to take pictures with the cars they knew they could never afford,” a young man who attended the exhibition told Al Jazeera. “The doors of the cars were locked too.”

5,2026-09-19,"You may be familiar with the various memes detailing the fact that once you reach middle age, you're automatically sorted, Harry Potter hat-style, into one of a handful of hobbies, such as sourdough bread making, gardening, or bird-watching. I can't contradict this, since I'm a middle-aged person who got sorted into bird-watching. But I do know that enjoying birds and their various activities is fun for all ages. Birds are beautiful, interesting, and unpredictable, and it's fascinating to keep a running life list of all the birds you've seen and hope to see in your lifetime.
Whether someone you know is in their bird-watching phase, preparing for this phase, or has been in it for decades, all of these unique gifts—for traveling bird-watchers and backyard bird enthusiasts alike—are things either I or another bird-loving Reviews team member tested, was gifted, or bought IRL and enjoyed.
For more specific equipment recommendations, check out our guides to the Best Smart Bird Feeders and the Best Binoculars. For other gift ideas, check out all our gift guide coverage, including the Best White Elephant Gifts, Best Gifts for Men, and Best Viral TikTok Gifts.
Updated September 2026: I added a birdseed subscription and a puzzle, and ensured links and prices are up to date.

Birdfy (Lifetime AI, No Solar)
If you love birds, nothing beats seeing them up close and personal, conducting all their birdy activities. I have learned more about birds in the past two years of testing smart bird feeders than I have over my entire lifetime, such as the fact that cowbirds will lay their eggs in other birds' nests (such as juncos) and the “host” birds will raise the cowbird chicks as their own, even if they look nothing alike. Or that jays and other corvids are scatter-hoarders and will spend an entire day picking nuts out of a feeder's seed mix to hide caches around the yard. There are many smart seed feeders on the market, but Birdfy's high-quality basic model (available with a blue or yellow roof) stands out for its balance of price, features, reliability, and usability without a subscription, making it a great gift option that I have personally given.
Lucy Hammett

My mom gifted this to my family about 10 years ago, and to this day, it is the single most-played and most-enjoyed game we have ever owned, in any genre. Shuffle the small cards and call them out; players place a blue cardstock square on their bingo card if they have that bird. My son loved it, his friends loved it, neighbors loved it, extended family loved it—it can be played by up to six people at a time, and is suitable for all ages. No reading or parsing of rules required. Best of all, neither my husband nor I minded playing it multiple times a day for years on end (a bonus feature important for any parent of young kids).

Experienced birders probably already have a decent set of binoculars, but for the birding-curious, kids, or someone just starting out, quality binoculars and a life list journal ($22) would make a great gift. WIRED contributing reviewer Caramel Quin declared these the Best Budget Binoculars. I bought a set for each member of my family for a cruise to Alaska last summer, and I'm glad I did. They're lightweight (7.2 ounces) yet sturdy, with an aluminum casing instead of plastic, and small enough to slip into a pocket if you'll be hiking and don't want to deal with them around your neck.

The one thing a bird-watcher could always use more of is seed, and this service offers a fun spin on what would otherwise be a boring gift. You can choose either a subscription or a one-time purchase of three boxes: The Starter consists of one 10-pound bag of seed custom-blended to appeal to birds in your recipient’s zip code. The Simply Seeds (pictured above) adds a 5-pound bag of a specialty blend (I received a fruit and nut mix), and the Splendid tacks on suet and mealworms. The blend for my yard—black-oil sunflower seeds, peanut splits, safflower, and millet—was spot-on; birds loved it so much there was little to no mess. (Though you can also specifically opt for No Waste, Hot Pepper, and No Starling versions of your blend.) Each box comes with a personalized letter from founder Susan Vandergriff about which birds to look out for in your area. This latter part needs a bit of fine-tuning, as my letter featured blue jays and summer tanagers, neither of which exist in my region, but I found the whole package charming nonetheless.
David Allen Sibley

This 8- by 11-inch hardcover by famed ornithologist David Sibley (known for his Sibley field guides) may be too big and too heavy to fit in a backpack for field reading, but it remains the definitive source for interesting bird facts. Did you know mallard nestlings have only a 15 percent chance of fledging, and that once they hatch, fewer than half of ducklings survive? And that jays in the Northeastern US often eat paint chips in search of calcium, which doesn't occur naturally in that region's soil? Or that chickadees specifically seek out spiders to feed their young for the first week after they hatch, as spiders are high in taurine? Whether you want to or not, you will learn all these things and more if you give someone close to you this book.
C&S
Amazon (Feeder)
Amazon (No-Melt Suet, 8-Pack)
My husband and I received this feeder basket and some suet cakes as a gift about 10 years ago. The basket has fallen on the ground countless times; been chewed on and used as gymnastics equipment by squirrels; and survived windstorms, snow, and pretty much everything Pacific Northwest winters have to offer. Sure, looks-wise, it's seen better days, but it still does the job.
Suet—beef fat, if we're being explicit; the solid-form cousin to rendered tallow—attracts a different variety of birds than seeds, primarily insect-eaters like woodpeckers and bushtits. It tends to melt and go rancid in the heat, so I like the melt-free hot pepper variety, which can be used year-round and has been deemed unpalatable by our neighborhood squirrels.
Moment
Moment (Lens)
Moment (Cases)
Moment (Mount)
Phone cameras keep getting better, but photos taken with that tiny lens aren't going to win you any awards. If you want more detailed and dramatic photos from your birdwatching expeditions but don't want to schlep a full camera setup around, try a professional-grade phone lens. WIRED reviewer Julian Chokkattu has tested several of these from Moment and recommends the 1.33X Anamorphic Lens for cinematic video, the 10X Macro Mobile Lens for close-ups, and the 14-mm Fisheye Mobile Lens for fun.
To use these lenses, you'll need a Moment Phone Case (available for recent iPhones) and, if you have an iPhone, the $5 drop-in lens mount in order to screw the lenses into the case. You will also want to use Moment's Pro Camera app, and Julian recommends this filter, which can help cut glare from bodies of water and shoot long exposures. Note that Julian doesn't recommend buying these lenses if you have a Google Pixel or Samsung phone, as Moment seems to have stopped making cases for Android.
Pop Chart Lab
Amazon
If you've ever wondered how many birds live in North America, this poster displays all 740 of them in one chart, both native and non-native, arranged by size and order. It's educational and a surefire conversation starter. In fact, I’ve had this chart framed in my home's entryway for almost eight years, and I don’t think a single person has visited my house for the first time and not commented on it, regardless of their ornithological interest level. It's really fascinating in person, and even if your giftee is already well-versed in birds, they'll probably still learn something new.
Jonathan C. Slaght
Amazon
Until reading this book, I had never heard of Blakiston's fish owl. And apparently neither had the author, bird-watcher and biologist Jonathan Slaght, until he caught sight of one—the world's largest living owl. What followed was a five-year passion project to catalog the owl in a remote region of eastern Russia. Part travel adventure, part scientific study, part memoir, this much-lauded nonfiction book reads like a novel and will be a page-turner for anyone who enjoys birds, exploration, or the outdoors.
Camojojo
Amazon (With Solar)
If your recipient has an existing bird feeder they like, or a seasonal nesting site somewhere around their home or property, they might love a stand-alone bird camera. This one is the best I've tested, compatible with both 2.4-GHz and 5-GHz Wi-Fi networks. The cute green owl face streams 4K HD video and pushes 1080p pics to the Hibird app. There's access to AI identification (which I found to be just OK), livestreaming, and Hibird's “Dr. Bird” ChatGPT-like help feature, which allows you to ask bird-related questions. (I didn't find it to be particularly helpful, but YMMV.)
The auxiliary solar panel can be mounted separately, or you can just take the camera inside to charge when the battery runs out, as I did—the battery lasts at least several weeks. The camera comes with a bracket and bendable arm for precise positioning, or you can jury-rig a custom solution. No subscription is needed, though a Hibird rep says that may change in the future.
Stonemaier Games
Amazon
Walmart
WIRED reviewer Simon Hill, our staff board game aficionado, says this award-winning card-driven game will appeal to anyone interested in birds, as it pairs strategy with plenty of educational information about different bird species. It can feel complex at first, so it's probably best for families with kids older than 10, but once you get the hang of it, it can be addictive. (Up to five people can play, but you can also play solo.) Playing time is 40 to 70 minutes.
Birdfy
Amazon
Birdfy
Lowes
Target
Contrary to popular belief, birds don't always sleep in birdhouses. Some cavity-nesting birds may use them for shelter during inclement weather, but where I live on the West Coast, birdhouses are primarily used for nesting, which happens during the spring. This is not my first go-round with a smart birdhouse; I tested the interestingly shaped Birdfy Polygon last year, which did not attract any birds then, and this year it kept going offline. As an alternative, I tried Birdfy's newer Nest Duo, which has two 2-MP cameras, one facing the entrance and one tucked discreetly inside. This one worked perfectly, both connection-wise and in terms of bird interest—mine raised a family of black-capped chickadees, and seeing their activity on the Birdfy app each day has been the most fun bird-watching experience I have ever had.
John Muir Laws
Amazon
Bookshop
John Muir Laws
In his guide to Gifts for Hikers, Backpackers, and Outdoorsy People, WIRED reviewer Scott Gilbertson said he loved this book by John Muir Laws (no relation to the famed naturalist John Muir) so much that it changed the way both he and his kids looked at the world, allowing them to see a level of detail they'd never noticed before. You don't even have to be an artist to appreciate this one, as it's more about the art of observation. He recommends including these pens with the book, which I have also owned in the past and love.
Nautilus Puzzles
Nautilus Puzzles (511 pieces)
If you're a puzzle enthusiast and haven't yet tried wooden jigsaw puzzles, you must. My go-to brand is Liberty Puzzles—the quality and designs are truly unmatched—but California-based Nautilus Puzzles is a close second. Especially appealing is Nautilus’ Parliament of Fowls, a visual interpretation of the 14th-century Chaucer poem. Though it's only 511 pieces, they are all individually shaped, many with a bird theme, which makes this puzzle both beautiful and extremely challenging.

Citi expects shortages across the global memory semiconductor market to deepen through 2031, driven by increasing demand for high-bandwidth memory (HBM), DRAM and NAND as artificial intelligence systems adopt continual learning.
In a research note published Monday, the investment bank identified continual learning as a potential central theme in AI development over the next five years. The approach involves updating AI models with new tasks and information while retaining access to previously acquired knowledge, creating demand for both memory and data storage.
Citi forecasts HBM bit demand will increase 62% year over year to 75.2 billion gigabits in 2027, followed by a further 69% rise to 127.0 billion gigabits in 2028.
The bank also expects demand for enterprise solid-state drives (eSSDs) to expand as AI systems require additional storage capacity to retain previously learned information.
For DRAM, Citi projects global demand growth of 30% in 2027 and 35% in 2028, reflecting the anticipated impact of continual learning on server DDR5 and HBM requirements.
Supply growth is forecast at 19% in 2027 and 22% in 2028, below the projected increases in demand. Citi estimates DRAM supply-demand ratios of -8.7% and -9.7% for the respective years, indicating a widening shortfall.
The bank anticipates a similar imbalance in NAND memory, forecasting demand growth of 29% in 2027 and 33% in 2028, compared with supply increases of 21% and 25%.
Citi projects NAND supply-demand ratios of -6.1% in 2027 and -5.5% in 2028, compared with an estimated -0.8% in 2026.
In its equity research, Citi identified Samsung Electronics 					(USOTC:SSNHZ), SK Hynix 					(NASDAQ:SKHY), Micron 					(NASDAQ:MU), Sandisk 					(NASDAQ:SNDK) and Kioxia 					(USOTC:KXIAY) as its preferred memory semiconductor stocks.
Within semiconductor equipment and materials, the bank highlighted Montage 					(USOTC:MNTCF), Applied Materials 					(NASDAQ:AMAT), Lam Research 					(NASDAQ:LRCX), TES, Eugene Tech and TechWing.
The projections reflect Citi’s expectation that AI-related memory consumption will grow faster than available supply, with the resulting market imbalance potentially extending through 2031.

7,2026-09-19,"Sam Altman, Jensen Huang and Tim Cook are expected at the state dinner on 24 September. The same executives were on the Beijing trip in May.
	Donald Trump and Xi Jinping at the welcome ceremony at the Great Hall of the People in Beijing, 14 May 2026.
Sam Altman, Jensen Huang and Tim Cook are expected at the state dinner Donald Trump is hosting for Xi Jinping on 24 September. The same executives were on the Beijing trip in May, where AI safety was discussed, nothing was signed and the US trade representative said chip export controls never came up. Five months on, Chinese customs is still blocking the H200s Washington licensed, and Beijing has called the industry’s own slowdown proposal fearmongering.
Xi Jinping arrives in Washington next week and the technology industry is going with him. Ashley Gold reported for Semafor that OpenAI’s Sam Altman will attend the state dinner Donald Trump is hosting on 24 September.
Nvidia’s Jensen Huang and Apple’s Tim Cook are also expected, according to Reuters and Bloomberg. There are separate discussions about a White House meeting with AI chief executives, which House Speaker Mike Johnson has referred to and his office declined to detail.
Trump and Xi met in Beijing in May, with Huang joining the delegation late and Cook also on the trip. AI safety was on the agenda then too.
What emerged was a discussion of collaboration on shared safety standards and no agreement. The US trade representative said afterwards that chip export controls were not discussed at all.
Five months later the same men are in a different dining room. It is reasonable to ask what the first meeting produced before assuming much of the second.
Washington cleared roughly ten Chinese companies to buy Nvidia’s H200, including Alibaba, Tencent, ByteDance and JD.com, with each approved buyer permitted up to 75,000 units. TNW reported that not a single chip had shipped.
By July a US trade official put it at very few, covering China and Hong Kong together. Chinese customs has blocked shipments, and suppliers of H200 components paused production as a result.
The deadlock is mutual and deliberate. American licences carry security and diversion conditions Beijing will not accept, and Beijing is steering its buyers towards Huawei and domestic alternatives regardless.
Huang runs the company whose China accelerator share has gone from almost everything to almost nothing. He has said he supports export controls while being accused in the Senate of lobbying to loosen them, and both things can be true of a chief executive with a market he cannot sell into.
Cook runs the company with the deepest manufacturing exposure in China of any American firm. Altman runs the one whose models are banned there.
These are the three executives with the most at stake in market access. Safety is what hangs over the visit, and access is what the attendees are there about.
Dario Amodei published his case for slowing frontier development on 12 September, and TNW covered the proposal. Its third step was eventual coordination with authoritarian governments, China included.
The same essay argued for maintaining restrictions on sales to China of the most advanced chips and chipmaking equipment, to widen the American lead. Beijing read that half.
Two days later a Chinese foreign ministry spokesman, Guo Jiakun, called it fearmongering, saying confrontation and vicious competition disrupt global AI governance. The state press described the essay as a veiled call to contain China.
Trump has cited the need to stay ahead of China as the reason to avoid domestic AI rules. That is the same argument in the opposite direction, and it leaves both governments treating restraint as a disadvantage.
Beijing has answered the slowdown proposal by calling it a Cold War script. Neither capital has adopted the premise that a joint brake is desirable.
The mutual suspicion now runs into the products themselves. The two countries have begun formally warning against each other’s AI models.
Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has signalled openness to discussing shared risks around AI with Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng, in talks in New York laying groundwork for the summit. That is a narrower thing than coordinated pacing.
Shared risks is the language of bioweapons screening and model misuse, which is where Amodei himself said limited agreement might be possible. It is also the only part of the agenda that does not require either side to give up an advantage.
Chips remain the currency. Washington has already used Nvidia allocations as a term in a peace agreement, so the idea of trading compute access for something else is established practice.
The obvious trade is access for assurances. Chinese buyers take delivery, in exchange for verification arrangements Beijing has so far refused as intrusive.
Nothing in the last five months suggests either side wants that trade at the price currently on offer. The dinner may be the moment it changes, and the record says to wait for the text.
Watch whether any chips actually move afterwards. A communique about safety costs nothing, and a customs clearance is evidence.
Watch whether the separate CEO meeting happens and who is in it. A room with Huang and Cook in it is about supply chains, and a room with safety researchers in it would be a different signal entirely.

Her approach is rooted in more than a decade of international experience and two master’s degrees, one in Sustainable Consumption from the Technical University of Munich and one in Consumer Affairs Management from Aarhus University.

8,2026-09-19,"AI is resetting assumptions about how quickly professional services firms should move, and consulting clients are getting impatient.
""I am seeing clients ask for our help in delivering outcomes at a more rapid pace"" compared to this time last year, Jeanelle Johnson, PwC's managing partner for the Washington DC area, told Business Insider.
""Something that we might have said takes eight to 10 weeks; the expectation is probably half of that. The shelf life is half,"" Johnson said.
Clients are not necessarily expecting an entire transformation to be completed in a fraction of the time; the expectation is more for pilots and proofs of concepts, she said.
""There's an expectation now that tools should accelerate the work,"" she added.
Johnson leads more than 2,200 consulting and accounting employees in her role as PwC's DC managing partner. Asked whether those compressed timelines were putting pressure on her consulting teams, she said: ""We're working on it.""
The Big Four professional services firms — PwC, Deloitte, KPMG, and EY — alongside firms like McKinsey, BCG, and Accenture, are at the center of the AI shift sweeping through workplaces. They have raced to build internal chatbots, train staff, and test AI in client work.
The technology can now assist with, and increasingly perform, early-stage consulting tasks such as market research, due diligence, and brainstorming.
At McKinsey, AI has accelerated the early problem-solving cycle, turning what used to be a team's ""week one answer"" — the first hypothesis after a week of research and analysis — into an ""hour one answer,"" Kate Smaje, McKinsey's Global Leader for technology and AI, told Business Insider in June.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
That frees up consultants to spend more time testing deeper parts of the issue tree rather than waiting days to get oriented, Smaje said.
Have a tip or story to share about working in consulting? Contact this reporter via email at pthompson@businessinsider.com or Signal at Polly_Thompson.89. Use a personal email address, a nonwork WiFi network, and a nonwork device; here's our guide to sharing information securely.
Amid the rank and file, employees at top firms have told Business Insider they're seeing a difference in delivery times due to AI.
A software engineer at Deloitte US told Business Insider that some coding tasks had fallen from a dayslong process to around five minutes with AI, and an EY-Parthenon consultant in the UK said that due diligence that previously took two weeks of manual work and spreadsheet handling can now be completed in a day with AI.
As well as pressure to deliver faster results, the shift is also raising questions about how the pricing model that has rested on human labor will adapt as more work gets done by AI.
For Johnson, faster delivery should not be confused with less work.
AI will change how consultants spend their time, freeing capacity for more meaningful or higher-value work rather than simply reducing the number of hours needed on a project, she told Business Insider.
The work itself is also becoming more complex, she added. Clients are navigating geopolitical tensions, wars, tariffs, and macroeconomic uncertainty simultaneously — a broader set of pressures than consultants would often have helped them tackle in the past.
""The confluence of all those things at the same time is something that we used to deal with one or two things at a time,"" Johnson said. 
""Now we're in a world order where we have to deal with 10 plus, plus, plus at a time.""

9,2026-09-19,"The longtime tech journalist says she’s seen Silicon Valley make the same mistakes again and again.
As the AI industry likes to tell it, superintelligent bots could one day cure cancer and end poverty. Or in the worst-case scenario, they might wipe out all humans from the face of the Earth. In recent weeks, Silicon Valley’s existential concerns about the technology it is racing to build have blown up. The tech journalist Kara Swisher sees it differently. “We have to stop thinking AI is going to kill humanity,” she told Atlantic executive editor Adrienne LaFrance at The Atlantic Festival. If the AI future goes sideways, bots won’t be to blame. People will be. “These are people at the helm of these things.”

In the several decades that Swisher has covered Silicon Valley—formerly as a reporter at The Wall Street Journal, now as the host of two podcasts—she says that she’s seen tech whizzes make the same mistakes again and again: failing to take proper precautions against the products they are building. “One of the things about tech people is they’re the smartest people in the world, in case you need to know, because they like to tell you,” she said. In her interview with LaFrance, Swisher also discussed the economic implications of the AI race, who is likely to win, and how Americans can respond to this dizzying and worrying AI moment.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.
Adrienne LaFrance: You have been paying attention to the tech world and AI for basically ever. And now it seems like within the past, say, two weeks, the rest of the world is suddenly paying much closer attention. So I wanted to start just by talking about where you think we are, in this moment.
Kara Swisher: I think the penny has dropped with a lot of people. Years ago I wrote Burn Book, which was tough on the tech industry. And at the time, they were sort of in an ascendant period, not as well liked as they started off. Someone came up to me and said, This book is really mean, as if I’m their friend or something. And recently that same person came up to me and they said, It wasn’t mean enough, which was kind of interesting. And I thought, Well, don’t worry; I’m coming up with a new one. It’s going to be great. The new book opens up with them standing with Donald Trump at the inaugural in front of the Cabinet.
LaFrance: By them, you mean this collection of tech leaders?
Swisher: It was Elon Musk; Sundar Pichai of Google, or whatever they call themselves now—Alphabet; Mark Zuckerberg; Jeff Bezos. To me, that was the sort of moment that you understood, because these are people who were not particularly political, including in Trump 1 … but then figured out that you can stick a coin in this president and get what you want, right?
So anyway, we’re at a moment where something’s going to happen. Something that I have said for a long, long time is that there are no guardrails on these people, ever. Not once. And there’s never been, in American history, except maybe the railroad expansion or some of the manufacturing expansion, or you go back to kids being here in New York City working in dangerous factories and things like that—there’s never been a moment where the people do not get a say. And these people get to make decisions for the rest of us that are really problematic, especially since seven people, five of whom have mental problems, and so with unlimited money, they do.
LaFrance: When you put it that way, it’s a little scary.
Swisher: It is a little scary. And the fact that we’ve allowed to do that. And they’ve also been able to capture politics, in a way, especially with Citizens United. But beyond that, the amount of money they have is astonishing. I always joke the deli on my corner has more legislation against it than most of these big companies.
LaFrance: And I want to get back to regulation, but you mentioned the railroads. What is the right historical analogy? I ask leaders in the tech world to help me understand how powerful this technology is and how consequential it will be relative to other major revolutions. And you get answers ranging from Oh, it’s the biggest thing since the internet to It’s the biggest thing since fire, right?
Swisher: AI has been around forever. Let’s be clear. This is not a new thing. If you look at early internet pictures, it’s laughable, when you look at early Yahoo or things like that. At the time when this was starting several years ago, I said This is a Cambrian explosion. This is a bigger deal than anything else because it reaches into everything.
LaFrance: Well, it’s interesting you put it in evolutionary terms.
Swisher: I think about it like that. And one of the lines I had in Burn Book was that everything that can be digitized will be digitized. And so the analogy I’m using right now is that we have to stop thinking AI is going to kill humanity. It’s like guns, you know, guns kill people. Like, no. People kill people, right? Or the car just ran into someone, got in an accident. These are people at the helm of these things, making these businesses and making a lot of money. That’s one thing. The analogy I use is nuclear energy, nuclear fission, like cutting up the bomb. And it’s the thing I said on Pivot today, which is they split the atom and, oops, it went boom. Oppenheimer knew it would go boom. They know what this is going to do. They don’t know everything. And they often do. One of the things about tech people is they’re the smartest people in the world, in case you need to know, because they like to tell you.
LaFrance: And they’ll remind you.
Swisher: And they’ll remind you. But then they’re like, We don’t know. That’s the thing that drives me crazy. And so I compare it to nuclear energy. Can you imagine if, when we were developing the bomb, it was private companies doing it? It’s the same thing. And AI is even more powerful, because it can set off a nuclear bomb. So let’s be clear of where it could be going.
LaFrance: I think the point you make is so important that we have to remember. That there are people using their own agency and power and wealth to make decisions that will affect everyone. Talk about those people for a minute, because you’ve known a lot of these figures for a long time, and I’m curious to hear. First of all, there is this sort of really interesting reordering of what even the major tech companies are. Like five to seven years ago, we would have maybe not even mentioned Sam Altman or Dario Amodei.
Swisher: Sure.
LaFrance: But talk about from what you’ve known of the folks you’ve known well and covered and written about over many years. How is Silicon Valley different? Does this flow from the original sort of pioneering spirit of Let’s build it because we can—and there is true optimism there, and there are potentially great uses for AI—or has something shifted in the culture of Silicon Valley?
Swisher: You know, I don’t know if there’s a culture there anymore. There was. And actually this week I interviewed Aaron Sorkin at a private event. I’ll have him on the podcast in a longer interview, but, about ex-Facebook people, watching The Social Reckoning, which is quite good. It’s not as good as the first one, but it’s good. It’s a really good movie. But I saw a lot of people and we were like, What happened to us? I saw a lot of people I covered, and it was a real community. It really was. And it was very exciting and fun and interesting. And everybody has scattered to the winds, essentially. The very wealthy people have insulated themselves in these places where they have an enormous amount of enablers. “Cashmere prisons” is what I call them. They have chiefs of staff and cars and this and that.
LaFrance: Right, it’s not like tinkering in your garage anymore.
Swisher: It never was. They love to do that. Look, I’m wearing a hoodie. I’m like, that hoodie costs $600. So I did one of my first stories for The Wall Street Journal about that. All the weird things they’d have to do.
We’re all equal. I’m like, why do you have controlling share? And they’d be like, Well, because to protect it. I go, yeah, but if we’re all equal, why aren’t we equal? They used to always lie to themselves almost continually. And now they’ve just figured out that all they have to do is hand money, and then they get what they want, or they don’t. So they have this idea of being simple people. And they’re not. They’re just not like you and I, and they pretend they are. I really do actually like Mark Zuckerberg doing his fighting, right? Fine. Whatever. It’s weird, but fine. I’m not going to judge. I do myself judge, but that’s okay. But I’m like, good. I’m like, good, good, good, great. You do that. But why do you need to do it on a floating thing in Lake Tahoe?
LaFrance: At least it wasn’t the White House lawn?
Swisher: That was fine. Whatever. I couldn’t get mad about that. I don’t give a fuck if he wants to do that. And then he hired MMA people. And, like, the MMA people have to pretend they’re having a good time. The whole thing, it feels Roman-like. And of course, he loves Rome.
He’ll probably think that’s a compliment, but it’s not. But it feels odd. And it’s accompanied by a feeling of victimization. How dare you not understand my genius? My famous parting with Elon Musk is he didn’t like a tweet I had. Then he wrote me, You’re an asshole.
LaFrance: What was the tweet?
Swisher: Elon’s going to Elon. I know, crazy mean. And since then, I have done a lot better. But it’s crazy. I have a heart filled with hate, according to him.
LaFrance: Let’s talk about the economy. So there are any number of scenarios that I’m really curious about. You hear people talking about how AI is entirely a bubble and it’s hype. Or, you know, especially recently after the Dario Amodei essay, this question of Oh, are they afraid of their own IPO? Or does the economy crash?
Swisher: It’s very confusing. But here’s the thing. I think they’re genuinely concerned. But why now? Because 10 years ago, 12 years ago, I’ve been at dinner parties where they talked about this concern. Sergey Brin, all of them. They were concerned then; they knew the atom would go boom when it blew up. They knew it. Now it’s happened. One of the things I didn’t think got a lot of attention that should have was the Hugging Face hack.
LaFrance: It has now.
Swisher: It has now. But at the time I was, like, what?
LaFrance: Two months ago or whatever, right.
Swisher: I think they were surprised by that. Although why would you be surprised? I tell this story again and again because I want people to understand. When Facebook came out with Facebook Live, I was in a room getting it demoed and I pointed out you could commit a murder on it. What are you going to do about that? What if people do this? What if people do this? What if bad actors get ahold of this? And they called me a bummer, like they never anticipated the consequences. Same thing here. What are you going to do when AI does what we think it’s going to do? And so they are genuinely nervous. Because now suddenly there’s liability, whether it’s Facebook’s $17 billion settlement—not enough, but better than $5 million, which was the last fine they got, essentially.
They’re starting to see, like cigarette manufacturers, that the lawsuits are piling up, whether it be chatbots, with kids or whatever. And I started interviewing the parents two years ago who were affected by these chatbots. And one of these guys came up to me and said, When are you going to stop interviewing the parents? I go, when you go to jail, I said, that’s when. This is criminal, what you’re doing, and you’re releasing a dangerous product without any kind of guardrails. But then the government, all our public officials, have let us down. I’m talking about the federal government. The states are trying. They have let us down. Right now, they found out. They’re nervous about it; I think they’re nervous about liability. That’s one. Two, the costs of this are insane. Like the internet cost a lot, but paid off. This is something else. There’s 10 frontier companies, whatever. Seven frontier companies. There’s going to be two.
LaFrance: Do you think that the two that survive are going to be among the seven today? Or is there some new player that’s going to emerge?
Swisher: Yeah, Google will be one of them. And then another, probably. I don’t know which one.
LaFrance: If you had to bet, which?
Swisher: I think they’ll merge. It’ll probably be Google and Anthropic. But I don’t know. ChatGPT—it’s too expensive, what they’re making. It’s not sustainable, and it’s not in the old-internet way of not-sustainable. This is disastrous. If e-toys went bust, who the fuck cares, right? But this is massive. This is trillions.
LaFrance: And it props up the whole economy.
Swisher: It props up the stock market. Trump has made a bet on AI that is very dangerous and it’s very fragile. And then it has companies next to it—Nvidia, Oracle, all those other things. And so there’s a financial worry they have. So they’re worried about liability and criminal liability. They’re worried about the stock market. And they’re desperate not to be blamed for what’s about to happen. And especially with the stock market—I’m amazed the stock market still hasn’t really taken the hit. It’s probably going to. Our stock market depends on seven companies. That’s not good.
LaFrance: I hate to make you speculate but I’m going to: So, when I think about this, there are any number of ways things could sour economically. There can be a regular old correction— maybe it’s a bubble, maybe it’s dotcom bust—that sort of thing. You could even imagine swarms of bots are starting to take over the internet, rendering the internet unusable, and then we can’t rely on the internet the way that we do and the global economy relies on. You could imagine a bank run because people are freaked out by AI. You could imagine a bank run led by AI agents because they’re the ones doing the trading. When you think through all the different scenarios that would not be good for the economy, what is the most likely outcome?
Swisher: All of the above. Any of the above. We already have a lot of threats to our infrastructure from foreign actors. This is us doing it. And one of the things that drives me crazy is it’s being fed on humanity. Humans with superhuman intelligence are going to do bad things, and that’s the one thing they don’t anticipate—technology that can be a tool, but it is always a weapon. There’s never a moment where it’s not both. Any of these scenarios are totally possible. And then who’s to blame? What happens? Who fixes it? What are we going to do to mitigate things like that?
Right now Trump is having a meeting. Guess who’s in the meeting? All the tech leaders. Are any critics, academics, scientists in there? I wasn’t invited to the White House, and neither was Scott Galloway. We have some thoughts of things you should do. We’re not experts, but we’re pretty good. They don’t invite anyone that would push back. Their favorite argument is that the reason we’re on top is because we’re unfettered. This is bullshit. They’re not the only innovative industry in our history. Cars were pretty cool. Light. I love electricity.
But these are things that happen at those times. So they act like they’re the only people who have invented things. I just watched the series on Ben Franklin. That guy invented a lot of shit. You know what I mean?
LaFrance: A top-tier guy.
Swisher: He really was, actually. A really important person. But they think they’re the only thing. That’s really astonishing that they don’t think that, Oh, we cannot be fettered. We cannot be fettered. Or even edited, when you read some of their essays. Mark Zuckerberg wrote an essay once. He sent it to me and goes, What do you think? I go, you’re in desperate need of an editor, but no one’s going to edit him.
LaFrance: Well, it’s funny you mentioned electricity. I’ve been thinking about that in this context, too, that the counterweight to the economic concerns that these companies will offer is that there will be massive productivity gains. But in terms of electricity, it was roughly 1880s that it comes along. It’s really not until the 1920s that factories adopt electricity and the rest of society is starting to see the gains that come from this new technology. Obviously, AI is moving so much more quickly in terms of improvement and eventual self-improvement. But on what timeline should we expect to see its greatest promises fulfilled?
Swisher: I think that’s something we actually don’t know. But I’ll tell you, the economy already sucks for most people in this country. Who got all the good bits? They did. Besides the Trumps, who got all the good bits? They do. Like, when are we going to get this stuff? Incomes are still flat. Years ago—and I tell this story a lot because it’s really important—I was talking to one of these people about income inequality, and I kept saying, if we don’t do something about income inequality, you’re going to have to armor-plate your Tesla. That’s where it’s going. And they built the Cybertruck. That was their answer. So it’s really kind of astonishing that they don’t think this is a problem. And they don’t think this is a problem because they live separately. They have a whole world. They have their own health care; they have their own mountains in Montana. These are not the people that are going to fix this thing. The last thing they do is the thing I have long called the “Xi or me argument.” It’s either China or us. I was like, not so good. Not a good choice for any of us. We have to come to an agreement globally. Trump’s about to meet with Xi Jinping. Zero is going to happen, because he’s not an Einstein, let me tell you. Everybody, I don’t know if it occurred to you, but Donald Trump is not Einstein. But we have to have a global cooperation with especially China around AI, and make decisions. And in China, they have more strictures on their AI than we do here, by far. And most people kind of like AI.
LaFrance: Well, they’re not allowed to not like things.
Swisher: I think we’re not allowed to not like things. I think we have to say, Oh, look, you’re wealthy and therefore you have the answer to everything. And you’ve noticed with these tech people, suddenly they’re experts on Ukraine; suddenly they’re experts on vaccines. At one point, I think it was Bill Ackman, who just gives me a headache every time I think of him. But come on. I’m glad he’s doing the brain institute. Good for him. But he did a whole thing on DEI. He was obsessed with DEI. They’re all obsessed with unfairness of other people. Whatever. Only people with a gun to the head of humanity would say they’re a victim, but he went on and on about DEI, and I think I said something like, I’m going to do a multipart series of tweets on hedge-fund investment because I know nothing. They think it extends to other things.
LaFrance: The hubris.
Swisher: Yeah. Can you imagine a Wall Street guy doing this? They’re just here to make money. If I leave you any message, they’re here to make money. That’s all they care about. They care about shareholder value. They care about protecting their stuff. They care about their weird beefs with each other. Because a lot of what’s happening in the AI thing, if you’re noticing all these different speeches by wacky Palantir guy, who is in very desperate need of a green tea—no, not a green tea. What’s a calming tea? Whatever. I was almost like, here’s a gummy.
LaFrance: Chamomile, someone said.
Swisher: He’s in desperate need of chamomile. They all have different opinions, because there’s all these beefs happening in their businesses and they act like that’s not the thing. If I leave you any message: They don’t care about you. They don’t care at all. And it’s not to say they hate you. They don’t care about you.
LaFrance: Let’s talk about regulation, because this moment has been interesting for me—this new push to pace the frontier, to slow the pace of AI development. People are seeing this as a novel thing in the tech industry. My somewhat cynical position, from someone who’s covered tech for a long time, is that we saw Mark Zuckerberg do the same when he realized he was up against the wall with Facebook, this posture of Regulate me, bro, please.
Swisher: Yes. Well, throw me in that briar patch.
LaFrance: Congress has no recent history of effectively regulating anything in tech. But what should happen? And are these calls for regulation even realistic?
Swisher: I know Congress has regulated things. They’ve regulated planes and trains.
LaFrance: In recent history. I mean, post-2000.
Swisher: They haven’t. And it’s because in the beginning, when they did Section 230, and I covered that for The Washington Post, they wanted to make it grow. Well, guess what? The baby is a fucking monster.
LaFrance: Section 230, for people who aren’t as much of tech nerds as we are, is the law that makes it such that the owners of social-media platforms can’t be treated as publishers, so they’re not treated as responsible for the material that they publish on their platforms.
Swisher: They can’t be sued. Now they are. Now what’s happening is two things that I think you should keep in mind. One is the legal stuff that’s going on—all this liability and product-liability and negligence stuff is starting to gain ground very much like the cigarette. You’re going to see tons and tons of that. That’s great. Let the lawyers after them. Our lawyers always do a good job at taking money from people.
LaFrance: And that’ll probably start at the state level. Don’t you think?
Swisher: It has started at the state level. But right now, one of the things they’re relying on is that the reason why they’re kowtowing to Trump is because they think he’ll do bad things to them. That’s why they’re doing it. That’s why you saw this rumor that—I urge you all to go see the Musk documentary. It’s terrific, by Alex Gibney. But Universal was thinking of not distributing it. They’re all trying to think of doing things to quiet him down. November comes, which—and I think it’s going to go the way everybody thinks it is. The Democrats are coming for them, and they think the Democrats won’t because they’re not crazy like Trump. One of them said to me, Well, the Democrats won’t be as mean to us as Trump. I said, they’re going to be as mean to you, and I’m going to be standing behind them yelling, you better be fucking mean to them, because they deserve to be regulated. They deserve it. And it’s better for them. It’s better for us, and we have to stop relying on them as magicians. They’re like alchemists. They’re not. They’re not smarter than you. They’re just richer.
We never had a privacy legislation in this country. We’ve never had algorithmic transparency. There’s like a dozen things we could do that would not hurt their businesses and would make us safer. And I think what they’ve done to us with social media, to our kids—and to us, by the way. It’s not just our kids who are; we’re all addicted, right? We’ve all been made addicted to this thing. And I think voters, and, I’ll finish with this, data centers, is a proxy. The anger over data centers, which is very real, is a proxy for the anger regular people know and feel in their own lives about what this is doing to them, and it doesn’t make them anti-tech. Tech is amazing. I love tech, but it doesn’t have to kill us, right? A 10 percent chance. Whatever it happens to be. The percent is actually 14.
LaFrance: I need to ask you one more question. We’re not going to let it end on that note, I need to ask you one more question. What are you doing right now? It’s such an uncertain time. What have you changed in your life to prepare for this moment? Are you buying gold bars?
Swisher: No.
LaFrance: No, but seriously, what? What are you doing? And what should people do? And how should we think about?
Swisher: Don’t buy gold bars. Don’t go there, okay? That’s bad.
LaFrance: Okay, sure. But is there anything we should do?
Swisher: She’s bought gold bars.
LaFrance: I don’t even know where to buy a gold bar.
Swisher: They’re also heavy.
LaFrance: So that was a bad example. But what should we do? What? Because the individual can feel like you’re up against this behemoth.
Swisher: But you’re not. This has happened before in American history. You are not up against this behemoth. They fall quickly. Trust me, they do. They’re very much bullies in some fashion. I’ll tell you two very quick stories. One of these people, Sam Altman, has had an apocalypse plan, and he told it to me years ago. This is 100 years. They all were into the apocalypse plan, which was weird to me. And he goes, This is what I have. I’ve got this place in Big Sur. I’ve got a motorcycle at my house, where I know where he lives.
LaFrance: He’s later said he regretted telling you this, by the way.
Swisher: Okay, so I’ll repeat it, then. I don’t really care. I don’t give a fuck. So he told me this whole plan and he goes, What’s your plan? I said, I’m going to kill you and take your things. And then he thought, She could kill me and take my things. And he hadn’t planned on that, genius that he is. And he’s a little guy, so I could easily take him.
LaFrance: All right. We should solve this nonviolently.
Swisher: Don’t do it. But don’t participate in their apocalyptic—We’re going to Mars. We have our bunkers. Fuck them. This is our world. You know what I mean? Don’t participate and let them run the fucking show. It’s yours. Go to your legislatures, push back. All these people across the country are pushing back on data centers. It’s going to matter. The only people I believe in in this country are voters. Like, even if they make bad choices.
LaFrance: Hear, hear.
Swisher: I think that we have to stop giving them this power, and demand of our public officials that they stop being essentially handmaidens to sedition. That’s what’s happened here.
LaFrance: I could talk to you all day, Kara. We’ve got to run.
Swisher: No gold bars.
LaFrance: No gold bars!

Of these, 10 stocks have gained between 40% and 180%, while four have more than doubled, turning into multibaggers and significantly increasing investors' wealth. The analysis also identified one stock that was newly added to the portfolio during the June 2026 quarter. (Data Source: ACE Equity, Trendlyne).

12,2026-09-19,"Football season is finally here — which means Sundays are once again all about touchdowns, trash talk, and a very serious spread of game-day snacks.
Whether you're locked in on the game or mainly showing up for Buffalo Chicken dip, ribs, and hot wings, you don't have to spend the entire day hovering over a hot stove to feed a crowd.
Business Insider spoke with cookbook authors Sarah Olson, who wrote ""The Magical Slow Cooker,"" and Kim Laidlaw, author of ""Everyday Slow Cooking: Modern Recipes for Delicious Meals,"" for ideas for easy game-day recipes.
These recipes can all be made in a slow cooker, allowing you to throw everything into your Crock-Pot and get back to the game.
Olson recommends her slow-cooker meatballs recipe for game day.
""I love grape-jelly meatballs, which only have three ingredients,"" she said. ""They're so good, everyone will ask you for the recipe.""
Another recipe from Spend With Pennies suggests placing your seasoned, raw meatballs in the slow cooker, topping them with sauce, and letting it finish them off.
Brats with onions, peppers, and garlic, simmered in the slow cooker with beer, are another of Olson's favorite game-day recipes.
""When the guests arrive, they get to choose when they want to eat since the slow cooker keeps these brats nice and warm during the game,"" she wrote on her website, The Magical Slow Cooker.
Plus, you can even choose peppers to reflect the colors of your favorite team.
""Brisket is a natural partner for the slow cooker, as a gentle braise renders it meltingly tender,"" Kim Laidlaw wrote in ""Everyday Slow Cooking: Modern Recipes for Delicious Meals.""
Laidlaw's recipe for slow-cooked braised brisket features garlic, onion, dry red wine, carrots, and either chicken or beef stock, accompanied by a chimichurri sauce.
""One dish I think is so versatile is pork shoulder in a slow cooker with broth and a little salt and pepper,"" Laidlaw said. ""I just braise that, and then you can turn it into so many different things.""
One of the best ways to use slow-cooked pork shoulder is to make pulled-pork sliders with all that tender meat. Then, top with barbecue sauce and slaw.
Olson's recipe for slow-cooked Buffalo chicken wings calls for thawed chicken wings, hot sauce, and unsalted butter. To get the wings super crispy, remove them from the sauce after 2.5 hours of cooking, then broil them in the oven before adding them back to the slow cooker.
One of the benefits of using a slow cooker is that you can start with a basic protein and jazz it up.
From Mexican-food-inspired spices to Indian simmer sauces, there are many ways to transform shredded chicken into something show-stopping by adding it to tacos, sandwiches, or salads.
If you want to add some carbs to your game-day menu, look no further than a piping hot pot of mac and cheese. Olson's recipe uses cream cheese, milk, sharp cheddar, and white sharp cheddar.
Martha Stewart's recipe for slow-cooker mac and cheese is also easy to make.
""It is important to use pungent cheeses, such as sharp cheddar, mixed with a little Gruyere or pecorino Romano for extra bite, since the white sauce and pasta will absorb a lot of flavor,"" Stewart writes.
One of the benefits of making a slow-cooker Buffalo chicken dip — or any other slow-cooker side dish — is that the recipe can be prepared well in advance and reheated when you're ready to eat.
""There are so many different dips that can be made in the slow cooker, you can make Buffalo-chicken dip, bacon-cheeseburger dip, cheesy bean dip, Rotel and Velveeta Dip, and even artichoke dip,"" Olson said. ""I like to serve all these dips with tortilla chips. Can't choose one dip? Make two or more for a variety for your guests.""
Spinach-artichoke dip is a game-day classic, and making it in a slow cooker keeps it extra warm, creamy, and scoopable from kickoff to the final whistle.
This recipe from Delish is made with full-fat cream cheese, shredded cheese, and mayonnaise,
""I often make a queso verde dip in my slow cooker for game days,"" Olson told Business Insider.
Her recipe for queso verde uses white Velveeta cheese, salsa verde, milk, and sour cream.
Spicy chili and chipotle turkey chili are ideal game-day recipes because they can simmer in a slow cooker for hours, letting the flavors deepen while you watch the game.
Serve with warm cornbread for a cozy, filling meal.
A DIY nacho bar is an easy way to feed a crowd while letting everyone build their perfect plate. It also puts those smaller slow cookers to work, keeping every topping warm and ready to scoop.
""I use three slow cookers for a nacho bar, which is a great way to use those smaller slow cookers,"" Olson said. ""I use one slow cooker for nacho cheese (store-bought), one for seasoned ground beef, and one for refried beans. I also make a toppings bar that has salsa, sour cream, guac, olives, onions, and tomatoes.""
