70,2026-09-19,"Yves here. This is a clever, well-written and persuasive article. The authors looked at job loss over a very long time, so as to include multiple waves of major technological change. It finds that most job types persisted even if the job content may have changed markedly for many, like accountants going from using green ledger paper to spreadsheets.
By William Skoglund, Post-doctoral researcher Uppsala University, Jakob Molinder, Associate Professor of Economic History Uppsala University and Kerstin Enflo, Professor in the Department of Economic History Lund University. Originally published at VoxEU
Forecasts of AI-driven job destruction rest on counting automatable tasks. But labour markets hire, pay, and fire whole occupations, not tasks – and occupations have proved far more durable than task exposure implies. Drawing on 140 years of Swedish census and register data, this column shows that roughly seven in ten workers today are in occupations whose core functions already existed in the late-nineteenth century. The mass-unemployment fear is overstated; the subtler question is whether today’s technological wave still generates the durable, specialised new work that earlier ones did.
Today, few economic questions command as much attention as how artificial intelligence will affect the economy and how it will reshape employment. The modern wave of alarm traces back to Frey and Osborne (2017), who estimated that 47% of US employment was in occupations at high risk of computerisation. Subsequent studies have been inspired by Acemoglu and Restrepo (2019), focusing on automation of tasks. Eloundou et al. (2024) find that large language models could affect at least half the tasks in around a fifth of US jobs; consultancies have produced their own automation-exposure maps; and the OECD has assessed how many high-exposure jobs were lost (Georgieff and Milanez 2021).
Much of this literature ultimately approaches automation through the tasks that make up jobs. Yet what technology can do to a task and what it does to a job are two different questions. A job is not a loose pile of independently substitutable tasks; it is a bundle the market prices as a whole. When some of a worker’s tasks are automated, the standard result is not that the worker disappears but that the job’s content shifts. Garicano et al. (2026) make exactly this point – AI redraws the boundaries of jobs rather than dissolving them – and the empirical record is by now familiar: AI changes the task content of work more than it eliminates the work itself. Humlum and Vestergaard (2025) find that, so far, AI chatbots have had remarkably small measured effects on earnings and hours.
If the labour market rewards occupations rather than tasks, then the right way to gauge technology’s true footprint is to ask how many people are actually employed in which occupations, and how that changes. That is the question we take to the long historical record.
What the Long Record Shows
In Skoglund et al. (2026), we trace the Swedish occupational structure from 1880 to 2019 using full-count censuses and administrative registers. We harmonise occupations across 140 years of data to a single occupational classification scheme (van Leeuwen et al. 2002). Instead of studying within-occupational task changes, we emphasise the function of an occupation. By this definition, a primary teacher from the 1800s has the same occupational code as a primary teacher from the 21st century, despite the tasks they perform having morphed over time.
We classify occupations by when they emerged: present before 1900, during the second or third industrial revolutions, or in the 21st century. Despite these successive waves of technological change, the bulk of Swedish employment remains anchored in occupations that already existed before the second industrial revolution. By 2019, as Figure 1 shows, close to 70% of workers were still employed in these incumbent occupations. The largest occupations today, such as nurses or retail workers, are, in their economic function, the very occupations that organised work in the 1880s, even though their task content has been transformed beyond recognition. The carriage driver became the lorry driver; the clerk acquired a computer. The occupation persisted; the tasks did not.
This is not a Swedish peculiarity. Replicating the approach on US data, we find that occupations new since 1940 account for only about 9% of 2019 employment. That sits in apparent tension with Autor et al. (2024), whose title-based ‘new frontiers’ approach attributes a majority of current US employment to specialties introduced since 1940. The gap is methodological: their measure infers employment from the share of new micro-titles, whereas ours counts the people actually in each occupation. Tellingly, when Autor et al. (2026) move to person-level census data, the picture shifts decisively toward persistence – they find 18.3% of workers in 2011–2023 employed in work introduced since 1970, far below the title-share estimates and much closer to ours. Measured in people rather than titles, the occupational structure is old.
Figure 1 Share of Swedish employment by technological occupational wave, 1880–2019
Where Durable New Work Actually Comes From
If occupations persist, where does new work come from? To understand this, we consider two distinct sources. Some new occupations are Schumpeterian – created directly by a new technology (electricians, computer programmers). Others are Smithian – created by the division of labour as markets grow and organisations get more complex (accountants, specialists, logistics workers).1 The general merchant of the colonial economy splits into shopkeepers, brokers and bankers not because trade is new, but because scale makes finer division of labour pay (Chandler 1978).
The crucial finding is that durable, well-paid employment accumulates overwhelmingly on the Smithian side. Smithian occupations are far more likely to survive: in our data, more specialised occupations are roughly half as likely to fall into decline, whereas occupations born in the most recent technological waves are several times more likely than incumbents to vanish – the punch-card operator being the textbook case. (It is membership in a recent technological wave, not a high ‘technology score’ as such, that carries the elevated risk.) The mechanism is a chain: technology creates scale, scale creates specialisation, and specialisation creates durable labour demand. We argue that the Schumpeterian sector raises productivity and enlarges the market but the employment it ultimately underwrites appears mostly elsewhere; as Smithian specialisation within enduring functions. This is also why the displacement fear might be overstated. The historical record shows that despite significant automation and technological change, occupations are strikingly persistent. And economic growth can still fuel the expansion of new Smithian jobs.
Signs of Decline
To understand where the labour market is headed, we gauge the age of Smithian and Schumpeterian occupations, building on the finding that declining occupations tend to also have an ageing workforce (Autor and Dorn 2009).
The very sector closest to innovation has matured significantly. Occupations born in the twenty-first-century’s digital wave remain a sliver of employment, and they are more volatile than their predecessors. Digital occupations have also aged. Computer programmers, once at least a decade younger than the average worker, have aged to roughly the workforce average – a striking convergence for what was once the youngest corner of the labour market, and the demographic signature of an occupation that has stopped pulling in the young (Figure 2). Engineers, by contrast, have held steady near the workforce average since the 1930s – the ageing is specific to the programming and operator occupations born of the digital wave. The youngest workers are increasingly found not at the technological frontier but in low-specialisation service work.
Figure 2 Worker age relative to the workforce average, selected occupations, 1880–2022
To further understand this, we trace earnings premia for different types of occupations. We find no earnings premium for being technologically new. The premium tracks specialisation: highly specialised occupations command large returns whether or not they are technology-intensive, and twenty-first century occupations pay no more than occupations inherited from the nineteenth century – slightly less. What the market rewards is the depth of the division of labour, not the novelty of technology.
What It Means for the AI Debate
So the genuine question is not the one dominating the headlines. It is not whether AI will displace workers from tasks – occupations, the unit the market prices, have historically repeatedly absorbed that kind of shock. It is whether the current technological wave will still do what earlier waves did: generate enough productivity and scale to spin off durable, specialised, well-paid new work. Earlier general-purpose technologies reliably converted innovation into broad employment through the division of labour. The digital wave, so far, has not visibly repeated the feat.
This shifts the AI and jobs debate from task exposure to actual changes in occupations and employment. Technology has historically changed jobs more often than it eliminated them, with much of the employment effect coming not from the new technological occupations themselves, but from the specialisation they made possible elsewhere.
That should lower the temperature on mass technological unemployment, but raise it on a quieter concern. The open question for AI is whether it can sustain the older bargain: turning productivity gains into new, specialised work through a deeper division of labour.
See original post for references

1770 – 1860 1st Industrial Revolution
(steam, steel, textile)
1873 – 1896 Long Depression
1870 – 1940 2nd Industrial Revolution
(chemicals, electricity, internal combustion engine)
1929 – 1939 Great Depression
1950 – 2000 3rd Industrial Revolution
(electronics, synthetic materials, atom)
2008 – 2023 Long Recession
2010 – ???? 4th Industrial Revolution
(genetic engineering, robotics)

I guess seasoned developers will come out in favor of “using Claude et alia as ‘coding assistants’” based on their assumption management will stop paying for and mandating the use of an integrated development environment (IDE)? If that is what you mean, does management typically back away from IDEs and push AI instead?
IDEs are supposed to provide a better way than the command line. Most of them are free, so there’s no serious cost issue with using them or not. The issue is that many of them are kind of bloated, providing a raft of features beyond the command line while not really improving productivity that much. E.g., JavaScript just sort of sucks, and using an IDE only makes it suck a bit less (the phrase “diamond-studded pig pen” comes to mind). Many developers are not against IDEs, but in general finding consensus around any particular toolset can be very difficult. So you will find teams that use VS Code or Eclipse by management decision, with individual team members who feel “meh” about it.
As “AI” enters the chat, all IDEs now have plugins that make it possible for devs to use services like GitHub Copilot. While the IDE may be free, serious use of Copilot is not. Many devs try these tools and suddenly report they feel more productive, but I think in part this is happening because Copilot is making short work of all the drudge that was needed due to bloat in the IDE or gaps in the language/libraries. That is, if the development environment and/or language of choice itself sucked less, Copilot wouldn’t feel like such a game changer.
Many shops really pushed the use of “AI” tools, only to get the bill a few months later and do a double take on “usage-based billing”. If the “AI” tools actually cost thousands of dollars per month per seat, where is the productivity increase? Why not just hire more devs? The teams themselves often feel chronically understaffed, as their ambitions for the project go beyond what they can achieve with current headcount. This led to situations in which management did a 180 on “AI” tools (e.g., Microsoft is a well-known example).
Still, many devs will say “I feel more productive” rather than questioning the IDE and/or language, and “AI” encourages management’s obsession with NOT hiring more ppl, so both management and engineering both want to believe that “AI” is really going to somehow change the world. Meanwhile, the research isn’t showing the clear productivity gains that everybody wants to believe exist.

71,2026-09-19,"One of the more impressive tent innovations to emerge from 2025, Cube Air Tent debuted as a spinoff of South African inflatable tent trailer brand Alphago. The brand quickly took on a life of its own, and while it always offered to ship globally, it's now made an official US launch, stocking its big, blow-up tents locally for faster, easier delivery at a lower price. And the brand plans to expand its lineup of air-filled tents and camping gear, adding all kinds of new products in the US, South Africa and beyond. 
No surprise that Cube hit the US market running with the product that put it on the map: the original Cube Explorer Pro Edition tent package. This model remains Cube's largest tent, and having checked it out in person at Cube's launch event at the recent Overland Expo Mountain West show, we can say it's definitely a big, breezy tent that invites you to spread out and get comfortable – regardless of whether you're standing around chatting at a trade show or hopping onto a mattress under the stars. 
The thing that struck us most about the seeing the Cube tent in person was its towering height – 6.6 feet (2 m) sounds high from the start, but it feels even higher when the peak is nowhere close to anyone's head. The floor, meanwhile, offers 107 square feet (10 sq m) of space, designed to accommodate between four and six people. 
That's not four people sardined together in alternating sleeping directions to optimize space, either, but two couples sleeping on their own cushy inflatable beds. Or you could set it up for seriously spacious solo camping like the guy below and live like a king camping in a foreign land. We reckon you could even fit actual home bedroom furniture in there without much of an issue:
Alternatively, you could set the Cube Explorer Pro up as a big, ol' sun shelter at the beach or park and enjoy the day without worrying about getting sunburned. With the available Turbo pump, it takes a mere four minutes to inflate those big inner air beams into full-standing form. Opt to go without that pump, and Cube still estimates a quick five- to seven-minute setup time with the included hand pump. So it's completely feasible to set it up quickly even if you're not spending the night. It includes a front awning that extends sun coverage outside the tent walls. 
While it's tall, broad and supported by air, the Cube tent is designed to stand up to wind and weather. The heavy-duty TPU inner air frame is designed for stability in wind and resistance to puncture, while the 150D Oxford outer fabric and 420D ripstop floor offer 3,000-mm waterproofing. 
The Cube tent can be packed up just as quickly as it inflates into form, and Cube notes that it's made the accompanying carry bag oversized by 30% to provide some room for error when squeezing, rolling, wringing and flying elbowing all the air out. We imagine it will take the uninitiated a little longer out in the dust and mud of the wild than the four minutes it took the Cube rep we watched do it on crisp, dry grass in a short video, but it's good to have goals to shoot for. And four minutes from wake-up to packed up is a worthy goal. 
Once packed, the Cube Air Explorer Pro weighs 66 lb (30 kg) inside the 38 x 26 x 20-in (97 x 66 x 51-cm) carry bag. That's not light by any stretch, but all that living space doesn't come without a serious amount of material. Plus, you're not meant to carry it far – from home to car, then car to campsite pitch, and then reversed. 
The Cube Explorer Pro is available for order in the US now for an advertised special early bird price of $1,999, a $400 discount off the $2,399 MSRP. And unlike prior to the company's official US expansion, you no longer have to work out shipping costs from South Africa. 
The Explorer Pro Edition is just the tip of the iceberg of Cube's American plans, as the company already has a whole list of ""coming soon"" products for the US market, as well as South Africa. Those will include the link modules that allow campers to connect multiple Cube tents together into a tent city, the Cube Solo inflatable swag-style tent, and the new Cube Duo two-person tent. We'll take a closer look at the newer products as they hit the market. 
Source: Cube USA

72,2026-09-19,"Unions warn that fuel subsidy cuts in the IMF deal could trigger renewed protests and rising costs.
Save
Share
Bolivia’s Congress has approved a $1.9bn International Monetary Fund loan in a major victory for President Rodrigo Paz, though trade unions warn the deal’s austerity conditions could spark renewed unrest.
Paz’s Christian Democratic Party does not hold a majority. But centrist and right-wing parties, which now dominate Congress after the long-ruling leftist MAS party was reduced to just two seats in the 130-seat lower house and none in the Senate, rallied behind the programme on Friday.
“We are finalising crucial agreements for Bolivia,” Paz said, warning that difficult decisions lie ahead as the Iran war pushes up global fuel costs.
“International prices are forcing us to make complex choices,” he added.
The loan, which is meant to fix a crisis years in the making, still needs approval from the IMF’s Executive Board before any money is released.
Bolivia once earned billions from natural gas exports, but years of underinvestment have caused production to collapse, leaving the country short of the dollars it needs to buy imported fuel.
To keep fuel affordable for ordinary Bolivians, the government spent heavily to hold petrol and diesel cheaper than even in Saudi Arabia, one of the world’s biggest crude producers, a subsidy that has since drained foreign reserves and fed a black market in smuggled fuel.
Under the IMF programme, Bolivia’s first multi-year arrangement with the fund since 2006, Paz must keep cutting those subsidies and reining in spending.
He has already raised fuel prices and plans to scrap the subsidy entirely by January. Officials say the deal should also unlock roughly $5bn more in financing from the World Bank and other lenders.
Paz, an ally of US President Donald Trump, called Friday’s vote a “historic step” for Bolivia.
The Bolivian Workers’ Central, the country’s main union federation, has denounced the plan, saying the cuts will push up living costs for struggling families, following weeks of road blockades in June and July that paralysed much of the country as protesters demanded Paz’s resignation.
Congress extended a state of emergency, declared to help clear the roads, for another 90 days on Thursday.

73,2026-09-19,"
  By Leo Morgenstern |
  at September 18, 2026 9:25pm CDT  
As the regular season winds down, MLBTR is taking a position-by-position look at the 2026-27 free agent market. Next up is the first base class. It’s not the strongest group, but the biggest names include a breakout bat, a former All-Star enjoying a bounceback campaign, and a superstar at the tail end of his career.
Player ages are for the 2027 season. Only players who appeared in MLB this year are included.
Previous entries in this series: Catcher
The Everyday Players
Bauers started turning heads last September. The Brewers were the best team in baseball, and the big-swinging lefty was their best hitter over the final month of the season. Heads kept turning as Bauers kept it up in the playoffs and into the following spring. Altogether, from September 1, 2025 through the end of spring training in 2026, he played 43 games. He slashed .392/.492/.794 in 122 PA. His 1.286 OPS led all major league hitters (min. 100 PA) in that span. Aaron Judge ranked second. Shohei Ohtani ranked third.
Still, it seemed like the other shoe was bound to drop eventually. Bauers was entering his age-30 season. He owned a .672 OPS and an 88 wRC+ in more than 600 regular season games. Even when he had the platoon advantage, his career numbers were mediocre. He struck out too much and didn’t make the most of his strength when he put the ball in play. Maybe he could be a productive platoon bat for Milwaukee, but no one thought he would keep pace with hitters like Judge and Ohtani much longer. Just to suggest that would have been ridiculous. 
Well, the Brewers have nine games left to play in the 2026 regular season, and if there’s another shoe coming, I don’t see it. With a 147 wRC+, Bauers has outhit both of the reigning MVPs. He has 26 home runs, 83 RBI, a 98th-percentile xwOBA, and a National League-leading .394 OBP. He ranks among the top 20 MLB hitters in wRC+ against right- and left-handed pitching (min. 150 PA). The only other hitter who can say the same is soon-to-be NL MVP Pete Crow-Armstrong. Once again, the Brewers are the best team in baseball, and Bauers has been their best hitter all season.
This winter, Bauers could make the case that he’s the best left-handed hitter available. That case will rely on teams believing he can sustain his 2026 success. His plate discipline, bat speed, and quality-of-contact metrics all work in his favor. Yet, one great season at 30 years old can’t erase hundreds of games of replacement-level production throughout his twenties. There’s no denying what Bauers has accomplished this year, but opposing pitchers will adjust, and he has to prove he can adjust back. 
That being said, as long as two or three clubs believe in him, a bidding war could help Bauers cash in on his breakout campaign. However, he might prefer to bet on himself instead. If he signs a one-year contract (or a multi-year deal with an opt-out) and enjoys another strong season, he could wildly increase his earning power for 2027-28. 
France has put together nearly as impressive a season as Bauers. The Padres’ cleanup hitter is likely going to finish a few plate appearances shy of qualifying for leaderboards, but if he qualified, his 144 wRC+ would place him among the top 10 hitters in the major leagues.
France has hit .299 with 20 home runs and an .879 OPS in 124 games. His numbers are equally impressive against right- and left-handed pitching. He’s also produced stellar defensive metrics for a second year in a row. All of that puts him on pace for new career-highs in fWAR and bWAR. In fact, Baseball Reference likes his defense so much that he ranks third out of all MLB first basemen in their version of WAR, trailing only Willson Contreras and Pete Alonso. 
Expected wOBA isn’t sold on France the way it is on Bauers. His .378 wOBA would be a career best, but his .333 xwOBA (66th percentile) isn’t far off from his career average. Unlike Bauers, however, France has had sustained major league success before. From 2020-22, France hit .285 with a 129 wRC+ in 335 games. In 2021, he was the Mariners’ best hitter. In 2022, he made the AL All-Star team. By fWAR and bWAR, he was one of the top 10 first basemen over those three years. 
Then, his numbers took a sharp turn for the worse from 2023-25. It got so bad in 2024 that the Mariners designated France for assignment before the trade deadline. During the next two offseasons, he could only land a non-guaranteed major league contract for 2025 and a minor league deal for 2026. 
Four years after his All-Star season, France has bounced back. When MLBTR’s Anthony Franco wrote about qualifying offer candidates for this winter, he included France (and Bauers) on the list. Admittedly, both were in the “long shots” category. Even so, just to be included in that conversation is a big deal for two players who joined their current teams on minor league contracts. Bauers has played parts of seven MLB seasons. France has played in eight. The qualifying offer (worth an estimated $23.1MM, per MLBTR contributor Ethan Hullihen) is nearly as much money as both have earned in their major league careers combined. 
Compared to Bauers, France should be more inclined to seek the highest guarantee he can command this offseason. He’s already 32, and it’s harder to believe he’ll ever be a top-30(ish) free agent again. The question is just how high that guarantee could be. Is a two-year deal with an annual salary near $20MM too much to ask for? Jorge Polanco is a pretty good comp, but with the way Polanco’s 2026 has gone, that might not be a comparison France’s camp wants to make.
The Aging Star
Goldschmidt isn’t mashing quite like Bauers or France. In 101 games as a part-time player for the Yankees, he owns a .773 OPS, a 114 wRC+, and 1.6 fWAR. At 39, the former MVP is still a solid hitter, but he isn’t the beast he once was. Same-handed pitchers have held him to progressively worse numbers over the last three years: a .675 OPS in 2024, a .619 OPS in 2025, and a .572 OPS in 2026.
Yet, put a lefty on the mound, and it’s like Goldschmidt hasn’t aged a day. In 175 PA facing left-handed pitching this season, he owns a 1.006 OPS and a 178 wRC+. Over the last three years, he ranks among the top five hitters (min. 300 PA) in OPS and wRC+ against southpaws, sitting beside names like Judge, Yordan Alvarez, Ketel Marte, and José Ramírez. Most hitters perform better against opposite-handed pitchers. That’s pretty much baseball 101. But Goldschmidt is taking that to an extreme. Watch him face a righty, and he looks like he should have retired two years ago. Watch him face a lefty, and he looks like he’s competing for another MVP.
An MLB team can only carry so many hitters. Most of them have to play a position other than first base or DH. All of them will have to face right-handed pitching at some point or another. That limits the value Goldschmidt can provide. However, for a team that can afford to roster a platoon first baseman/DH, a player like Goldschmidt can be a true luxury. 
From Goldschmidt’s perspective, it’s hard to see why he wouldn’t want to play at least one more year. Some will call him a future Hall of Famer already, but others would say his case is borderline. Another season would likely get him to 400 homers and bring him into the top 100 on the all-time RBI leaderboard. There’s also the ultimate prize to consider; Goldschmidt has yet to win or even play in a World Series. That could change this fall, but if it doesn’t, he’d have all the more motivation to continue his career. 
To do so, he’ll need to find a team that’s willing to pay for his particular set of skills. Considering the year he’s had, the weak free agent market, and the star power still attached to his name, I doubt that will be a problem.
The Part-Time Bats
Bell has qualified for the batting title in each of the last 10 seasons, but he’s in the part-time category here because he’s played most of his games at DH in three of the last four years. Presumably, he could manage a more regular role at first if that’s what’s asked of him, but his -46 DRS and -35 FRV both rank last among first basemen since 2016.
On offense, his .735 OPS and 105 wRC+ this season don’t jump off the page, especially for a first baseman or a DH. Indeed, Bell hasn’t been a true impact bat since his twenties. However, he has been remarkably consistent, with a wRC+ between 102 and 106 in each of the last four years. He’s also a switch-hitter who offers slightly above-average production versus righties and lefties alike. All this to say, you know what you’re going to get with Bell. He might not be an impact bat, but he won’t be a liability either.
For most of his career, Bohm has been Philadelphia’s primary third baseman, though he has never graded out well there. He’s also played a fair amount of first base, and it’s been his regular position since the Phillies switched up their defense at this year’s trade deadline.
Bohm has had some great stretches over the years, including a 44-game debut in 2020 that earned him Rookie of the Year votes and a dominant start in 2024 that earned him an All-Star nod. Yet, he’s gone through just as many rough patches, and ultimately, he’s never proven he can be much more than a league-average bat. That’s barely been enough to cut it as a poor defensive third baseman, and it’s really not enough to cut it as a poor defensive first baseman. So, we have Bohm listed as a part-time bat, even though he’s been a full-time player for the past several years. His career stats against left-handed pitching (.856 OPS, 130 wRC+) will play at any position, but he needs to hit righties better to warrant an everyday role at first base.
Lowe has been an above-average hitter in seven of his eight big league seasons. From 2019-24, he hit .272 with a .789 OPS and a 123 wRC+. He won a Silver Slugger in 2022 and a Gold Glove in 2023. Perhaps the most impressive aspect of his profile was that he didn’t have platoon splits. The list of left-handed hitters with meaningfully better numbers than Lowe against left-handed pitching in that span was short.
Then came 2025. Lowe’s numbers were down across the board, but his major problem was that same-handed pitchers had him figured out. He owned a career-worst .665 OPS and 84 wRC+ when the Nationals released him in August. The Red Sox scooped him up and shielded him from southpaws. That helped Lowe salvage his season with a .790 OPS and 115 wRC+ down the stretch. Even so, he had to take a minor league contract with the Reds this past winter – and no one was particularly surprised. As well as he played for the first several years of his career, he finished 2025 with the lowest OPS, wRC+, and xwOBA in the majors (min. 150 PA) against left-handed pitching. Simply put, platoon-only first basemen aren’t a very valuable commodity (especially when they aren’t Paul Goldschmidt).
Lowe has rebounded in 2026, to the point where a major league contract seems like a reasonable goal for the offseason. His numbers have only gotten worse against lefties, but fortunately, he hasn’t had to face them all that much. Meanwhile, his .840 OPS, 128 wRC+, and .350 xwOBA against righties are all above his career averages. Lowe isn’t a full-time player anymore, but he’s a safe bet to produce against the kind of pitchers who throw more than 70% of big league innings.
The Bench Bats
Hoskins was a middle-of-the-order bat throughout his Phillies tenure. In 2023, he lost the entire season to a torn ACL, and he hasn’t been the same hitter since. Over 316 games dating back to 2024, Hoskins owns a .719 OPS and a 102 wRC+. He still has pop and knows how to draw a walk, but swing-and-miss has become more and more of a problem. His high strikeout rate has started to outweigh the things he does well. A right-handed hitter, Hoskins can’t even make the case for himself as a lefty masher à la Goldschmidt; over the last two years, Hoskins has actually struggled even more against opposite-handed pitching (.683 OPS, 90 wRC+). Like all the other names in this category, it’s hard to believe he’ll see any guaranteed contract offers this winter.
Smith has played for four teams over the last three seasons. None of them has let him face many southpaws. His bat is roughly average when he has the platoon advantage, and it’s much worse when he doesn’t. Accordingly, he had to settle for minor league contracts with each of those four clubs. Despite that, he’s made it into 263 games since 2024, including 105 (and counting) this year. There’s no reason to think this pattern – minor league contract, part-time major league role, decent splits against right-handed pitching – won’t continue in 2027. We’re talking about a role player here, but one who has found consistent playing time for himself over the last 10 seasons.
In his age-34 season, Vargas has set new career-highs in games, plate appearances, and most counting stats. However, following an absurdly hot start to the year – he had a 1.131 OPS and 208 wRC+ through his first 100 PA – he has regressed to his old self. The switch-hitter makes a ton of contact, but he doesn’t swing hard enough for that contact to make an impact. As such, his overall offensive numbers have been league average in each of his 10 big league seasons. To that point, his bat doesn’t belong at first base, and he’s only on this list because the D-backs haven’t had any good options at the cold corner this year. To Vargas’s credit, he has earned +7 DRS and +3 FRV in his first season as a primary first baseman. Yet, the fact that he can play all over the infield, and even the outfield in a pinch, is the reason he’s been playing in the majors for a decade.
Wade has played most of his games in the outfield this year, but he was primarily a first baseman in the three seasons prior. After a shockingly poor 2025 (.524 OPS, 53 wRC+), the lefty batter has produced much more respectable numbers as a bench bat for the Astros in 2026 (.681 OPS, 96 wRC+). The version of Wade that hit at the top of the lineup for the Giants from 2021-24 might be gone forever, but his eye is still sharp, and his plate discipline is a genuine asset. That discipline should land him a new minor league deal and an invitation to spring training.
Retirement might be on the horizon for Santana. An adductor strain limited him to eight games with the Diamondbacks at the beginning of the season. He has since been released from contracts with the D-backs and the Braves, and he’s been a free agent for over a month. As telling as that might seem, Santana hasn’t given any indication that he plans to hang up his hat. He’s only two years removed from a strong 2024 campaign, in which he hit 23 homers, posted a 113 wRC+, and won the AL Gold Glove at first base.
Tellez has only played seven games in the majors this year, but he’s put up solid numbers at Triple-A in the Braves organization (.825 OPS, 117 wRC+). Over nine MLB seasons, he’s been a league-average hitter, with plus power from the left-hand side that cancels out his poor on-base skills. He’ll be in the market for another minor league contract, but maybe he’ll find one with a team that’s more likely to give him a shot in the show.
Meneses burst onto the scene with a red-hot run for the Nationals in August and September of 2022. Then, his numbers took a steep downward turn in 2023 and ’24. He’s played almost exclusively at Triple-A over the last two and a half seasons, though he made his way into nine games for the A’s this summer.
Club Options
The chances that the Orioles pick up Mountcastle’s $7.5MM club option for 2027 are slim. He has followed the worst season of his career in 2025 with another injury-marred campaign in 2026. The righty batter put up a .653 OPS and 82 wRC+ in 89 games last season. This year, he’s 4-for-14 in eight contests and hasn’t played at all since early April. From 2020-24, Mountcastle was a solid hitter, especially against left-handed pitching. Yet, even at his best, he was closer to “average” than “star.” He’s the longest-tenured player in the O’s organization, but if they want to keep him around, they should be to do so for a much lower price than $7.5MM.
To sum up….if you need a first baseman…you’re screwed.
The Brewers make average players better. They turned Andrew Vaughn’s career around in short order last year, and he’s hitting over .300 this year.

BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
Topic:Music Industry
Chris Wallace says The Black Wreath was the place that ""made us who we are"". (ABC News: Xavier Martin)
Tell us about your experience. 
It might seem unlikely that a volunteer-run venue in one of the country's most remote towns could inspire people from across Australia and the world to help raise $1 million to save it.
But it did.
Over the past month, the founders of popular Alice Springs live music venue The Black Wreath have been fundraising to buy the building and turn the whole block into a creative hub after their landlord put the property up for sale.
Artists and music industry figures came on board, including Amyl and the Sniffers, who played at The Black Wreath's Blacken Open Air festival in 2022, as well as donors who matched pledges dollar for dollar.
The Black Wreath hosts local gigs and offers artists a recording and rehearsal space, helping kick-start the careers of some of the most remote bands in the world.
Before he launched the campaign on August 4, the Black Wreath's founder, who goes by the mononym Pirate, hardly used social media.
But by the end of the appeal, he was posting daily videos, pushing as hard as he could to save one of the creative heartbeats of Alice Springs.
""It's a massive milestone, I'm really proud of the community achievement and everyone that's gotten behind this,"" he said.
The fundraisers gave themselves until September 13 to reach their $1 million funding goal, but hit the target two days early before a massive weekend of coordinated gigs across the country in solidarity with the cause.
Pirate says he hopes to run The Black Wreath as a foundation. (ABC News: Xavier Martin)
It wasn't until the day after the Alice Springs gig, headlined by South East Desert Metal, that Pirate had his first day off in months.
He said the whole town ""became a metal head for the night"".
""The last month it's been 12 to 15 hours every single day, it hasn't been healthy, but it's been necessary,"" Pirate said.
However, Pirate said there was a long road still ahead.
As part of the fundraising campaign, people from across the country made pledges for something in return, which ranged from T-shirts, records, rings and hats to an entire festival, Blacken Open Air, to be held on April 30 next year.
""I'm not celebrating yet just yet; I'm getting back to work,"" Pirate said.
""It's just the beginning of the process, we've raised some money and now we need to negotiate with the owners.
""There's a lot more to do.""
Pirate said the grand vision was to turn the entire property into a creative precinct.
""My big hopes and aspirations and dreams are that it's run as a foundation and the income generated goes into local music and arts,"" he said.
""And touring Central Australian artists outside of here, [as well as] bringing artists to Alice Springs and putting them on stages here as well.
""Fifteen years of doing this, I find that the travel aspect has been the biggest hindrance to connecting us with a wider community.""
Ltyentye Apurta/Santa Teresa musicians South East Desert Metal describe themselves as the most isolated touring band in the world, and credit The Black Wreath for success that's seen them perform on the steps of the Sydney Opera House.
The band's lead vocalist and guitarist, Chris Wallace, said there was nothing else like it in Alice Springs.
The fundraiser to save The Black Wreath has been hailed as ""an amazing moment for the whole nation"". (ABC News: Xavier Martin)
""I really love it, all that comes from Black Wreath,"" he said.
Reflecting on the successful fundraiser, Pirate said it was ""an amazing moment for the whole nation, especially for the heavy metal community"".
""This is very historic,"" he said.
""I'm proud to be a part of that community and we're as far away from everyone as you can possibly get, but right now it feels like there's no distance at all between us.""

80,2026-09-19,"Gas prices seem to have gone up so much that even the most responsible drivers have considered cutting it close to the ""Low Fuel"" alert before heading to the station. 
Watching the numbers multiply on the pump meter is a special kind of pain, but context is everything. Back in 1956, gas was a mere 30 cents a gallon. That sounds like a steal until you run it through an inflation calculator: in today's dollars, that gallon cost $3.69. Fast-forward through the 1970s energy crisis, the 2008 economic collapse, and recent market shocks from the Russia-Ukraine war and Middle East conflict, and by September 2026, AAA placed the average price of a gallon at approximately $4.44.
Of course, national numbers tell only a fraction of the full tank's story. State tax rates, environmental rules, and shipping distances mean a driver in California routinely pays more than someone filling up in Texas. Nonetheless, the data on average annual retail fuel prices used for this list come from the U.S. Department of Energy and the U.S. Energy Information Administration, with historical figures adjusted to 2026 dollars.

Daddario has been rocking a lot of black lately, and I'm here for it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After a brief foray out of the fashion game as she settled into life as a mom, Alexandra Daddario is back with a vengeance. Beyond her cute cutout dress and end of summer bikini pics, the mom of 1 has been all about black dresses lately. I’m still thinking about the long, black backless gown she coolly wore for the AMC Upfronts ahead of Mayfair Witches coming in 2027. She then wore a black belted look for the SAG-AFTRA Foundation Conversations Presents ""Couples Weekend” event. Now, she’s back in a little black dress in her latest Instagram post.
Daddario showed off her most recent celebrity fashion moment, a black draped little black dress which she paired with a red lip and jewelry from Sharra Pagano. The folds on the black dress and the earrings are super dynamic, and I really wish I could have seen the dress from the back, because the front is so unexpected.
A post shared by alexandra daddario (@alexandradaddario)
A photo posted by on 
Daddario has never had the flashiest wardrobe in Hollywood, but she’s really good about finding clothes that fit her well, and feel approachable to boot. They usually are a little too nice to be something her fans could wear out on a weekday, of course, but I do like how everyman the color palette she typically chooses is. She likes a bold lip and her eyes are so bright (her son’s match!) that she doesn’t need a lot of punch when it comes to dress color.
We'll doubtless see more from her as she promotes her upcoming movie Hershey (you can guess what that's about) and the latest season of Mayfair Witches coming down the pipeline.
To note, the black dress is always a staple and a classic in Hollywood. Just this year, Halle Berry rocked a cool knitted dress for her 60th birthday. On carpets, Hannah Waddingham rocked one (for a very empowering reason), and Sydney Sweeney also wore a pretty, one-shoulder little black dress to celebrate the Golden Globes. Megan Fox is honestly in black a lot, but she did prove she’s the queen of captions with her latest LBD look, as well.
Obviously, Daddario is in good company with both the black dresses and the funny captions. Her fans also had a good time with her “ready for the supermarket” joke.
In short, I guess watch out Megan Fox, as Ms. Daddario seems to be developing a knack for fun captions. You may be ceding that Instagram caption title soon… though there’s always room for two queens.

Bank of America (BofA) has introduced a 12-month target of 7,800 for the S&P 500, implying approximately 2% upside from current levels, while raising its year-end forecast and identifying several risks to US equities.
In a note published this week, strategist Savita Subramanian increased the bank’s year-end target to 7,400 from 7,100. The revised projection implies a decline of approximately 3% from current levels.
“Our 12-month target of 7800 is nothing to write home about, suggesting +2% from here,” Subramanian wrote.
The strategist expects investors to have an opportunity to enter the market at lower levels, citing the relatively limited number of market pullbacks recorded this year.
According to Subramanian, the S&P 500 has experienced only one decline of at least 5% this year, in March, compared with three such declines in a typical year. She also noted that approximately half of BofA’s indicators associated with bear markets have been triggered.
The bank identified inflation, Federal Reserve policy, earnings quality and credit conditions as potential sources of market risk. Subramanian said current price-to-earnings valuations imply an inflation outlook considerably below the bank’s own forecasts.
She also referenced the 1970s, when upside inflation risks, dollar depreciation and Federal Reserve interest-rate increases coincided with an equity market decline exceeding 40%.
On US politics, Subramanian described concerns about a Democratic sweep in the midterm elections as a “red herring” for capital expenditure, arguing that state governments are largely responsible for driving the artificial intelligence infrastructure expansion.
Despite the near-term risks, the strategist maintained her longer-term positive outlook for equities, citing potential productivity improvements as businesses replace labour-intensive activities with scalable processes.
BofA forecasts S&P 500 earnings growth of 33% in 2026, followed by a further 12% increase in 2027.
Within equities, the bank expressed a preference for large-cap value stocks, selected small- and mid-cap companies and the equal-weighted S&P 500 over the traditional market-capitalisation-weighted index.
The forecasts indicate that BofA expects limited index-level gains over the next 12 months, alongside differences in performance across market segments.

84,2026-09-19,"The release of DOOM in 1993 revolutionized PC gaming, and its full source code followed a few years later. Anyone can look up the code, modify it, and get it running on any kind of hardware (or even software!).
And I do mean any. There's been a sacred ritual of making DOOM run on the wackiest, most impossibly constrained pieces of hardware, with several that come to mind that really make you stop and think, ""Just how on Earth?!""
Every console I love, all in one place.
Yes, you read that right. Someone got DOOM running within a pregnancy test kit, complete with an awful monochrome OLED display. It becomes a lot less impressive once you realize that this design involves almost none of the original silicon of the actual test kit.
Both the original LCD module and CPU were nowhere near enough to run DOOM (with the LCD module being only able to display 4 symbols). As such, Foone Turing had to replace both with an Adafruit Trinket and a small OLED display. And it worked!
DOOM ran on a pregnancy test kit, on that eye-squinting monochrome OLED display (with much difficulty). You’d also have to use a Bluetooth keyboard or controller, since there was no way to map the buttons to something.
As digital cameras became common, a transition toward “smarter” devices was also underway. The idea was to cram as many software features as possible, letting the user edit the photo on the go without any computer to plug the camera into.
This mandated some powerful hardware, with the Kodak DC290 Zoom being notable for having a PowerPC CPU with 8 MB of RAM; this essentially made it a mini PC that could run real code. Running DOOM on these cameras was pretty straightforward, but involved specialized ports.
Most of what you’d expect works, including sound. Amusingly enough, the camera buttons are all mapped to the controls, letting you control the game using just the camera itself.
It’s a weird way to play for sure, more so thanks to the clunky control scheme.
It might seem cool (pun intended) to see DOOM running on the small panel of your smart refrigerator, but the novelty quickly wears off once you realize that it’s essentially streaming the game.
Nope, it's not a bare-metal port, which is a bit of a shame since the hardware is certainly quite capable. Since these smart fridges use Tizen OS, it is possible to just port over the Xbox Game Pass Ultimate app, assuming you have an active subscription, and stream any version of DOOM onto the screen.
Or you could just cast your phone’s display and stream a run of DOOM. It’s quite a lot less impressive when you take the whole streaming thing into account, but it does make for a fun party trick.
DOOM on calculators has had a long lineage of ports, with the TI graphing calculator being the first of many such examples. While more modern calculators run fully fledged ports of the game, they also have a lot more computing power to spare.
The very first DOOM ports for these calculators had to deal with as low as 3 KB of RAM and a very weak CPU that boosted to single-digit MHz speeds. This meant that these weren’t true ports, but very rough approximations of the original game. Still, that’s as far as you’d get with an old monochrome display, and it technically still is DOOM, in a way.
Modern iterations are fully featured, with sound, sprites, and the full set of levels. However, running DOOM on newer calculators has unfortunately become a bit of a challenge, since these devices tend to have some tighter security.
You’d need a very specific electric toothbrush to do this — quite obviously, you'd need one with a display and some actual processing power. The most notable example would be by Aaron Christophel using the Evowera Planck Mini.
Essentially, he abused the toothbrush’s OTA update mechanism to flash custom firmware using an ESP32 board. This eliminated the need for any teardown and used a specialized port of DOOM, stripped down to fit the whole game engine and WAD file within 4 megabytes.
There was unfortunately no way to play the game using the default “toothbrush controls”, and so Aaron chose to pair a Bluetooth mouse to the toothbrush, which is as ridiculous as it sounds.
The Neo Geo is a bit of an oddity, in more ways than one. Initially released back in April 1990, this console was a 2D beast, capable of rendering detailed sprites. However, DOOM takes place in a 3D space, and the Neo Geo has historically been incapable of running traditional 3D games.
Yes, there have been a few outliers, but these were never true 3D, using sprites to simulate a 3D space (with many, many restrictions). This made a port of DOOM impossible to pull off, but modders have (to the surprise of no one) found a way.
One of the first real attempts was porting DOOM64KB, a highly compressed version of the game. While the E1M1 map works in its entirety, it renders at an impossibly low resolution, which doesn’t look as good. Still a mighty impressive port regardless.
There is another, more complete port known as DOOM-NG. It uses a sprite-based approach to render the game one frame at a time, which is admittedly a bit choppy. However, this is the full E1M1 game map running on real Neo Geo hardware (with some tricks), and that running, despite those limitations, is a miracle in and of itself.
There are two kinds of printer “ports.” The first is a straightforward port of the game to modern smart printers. Most printers come with some sort of compute, and often a small LCD display. These ports run on that tiny display, and they run pretty well.
The second, more intriguing “port” (port is stretching the term) runs DOOM on an actual old-school printer that uses thermal receipt paper. Achieved by YouTuber Bringus Studios, the game prints out each frame of the game in sequence, making it into a weird turn-based take.
Admittedly, while it’s a bit of a novelty (and in no way playable), it still is impressive for the effort undertaken alone.
 The Commodore 64 was discontinued in 1993, the same year DOOM shipped. This made it widely out of date and leagues behind the relatively high PC system requirements mandated by the game.
This made porting DOOM to the Commodore 64 a real challenge, but walls do break down, eventually. Three distinct attempts have run DOOM on the Commodore 64.
The first involved a Raspberry Pi Compute module doing all the CPU-bound heavy lifting (with other functions offloaded to the Commodore 64) in a kind of DIY expansion unit. This made it run surprisingly well, but it was in no way a true port.
Another native port comes in the form of the SuperCPU port, which mandates the SuperCPU hardware upgrade path for these machines. Simply put, you’ll require a rather rare piece of old electronic equipment to be able to run the game at all.
The third and final iteration comes courtesy of Steve McCrea, with a native Ray-Casting port for DOOM. This is designed to run on stock Commodore 64 hardware with zero expansion cards in use.
This port is built on his own raycasting engine, which makes it doubly impressive. Levels E1M1 through E1M8 are fully playable, albeit without any music to speak of. This undertaking took some serious work and is perhaps the most impressive port in the list.
There’s just something about making impossible ports possible. Call it a habit of tinkering, or an attempt to venture out into the unknown; it’s always a fun hobby to engage in.
There's always been a conscious effort to port DOOM to every kind of hardware imaginable, and that is partly possible because its entire code is open source. That being said, porting DOOM is a skill, especially for obscure hardware, and if nothing else, is a programmer flex.
It also speaks volumes about the game itself, which has stayed relevant since its 1993 PC release. After all. DOOM did pioneer the first-person shooter genre, and it's still a great way to step into the world of game dev.
SteamOS is ol' reliable when I want a Linux-gaming experience at this point.

Mouse X2 will not be sold outside Japan

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Japanese electronics maker Mouse Computer has introduced a compact laptop called the X2, a compact notebook weighing 981g.
The device pairs a 12.2-inch display with AMD processors, offering configurations that scale from modest to genuinely capable performance.
The laptop frame measures 277.1 mm wide, 210.2 mm deep, and 19 mm tall, retaining a single unoccupied SODIMM slot for later RAM upgrades.
Buyers can choose between three distinct AMD APUs, each aimed at a different performance and price bracket for portable computing.
The base option, a Ryzen 5 216, combines two full Zen 4 cores with four smaller Zen 4c cores in a six-core layout, which should perform similarly to AMD's existing Ryzen 5 8540U based on available benchmark comparisons circulating online.
A step up brings the Ryzen AI 5 340, part of AMD's Krackan Point lineup, also arranged in a six-core configuration.
Multicore results suggest this chip lands close to Intel's Core Ultra 7 165H and edges past the Core Ultra 5 325.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
The top configuration, a Ryzen AI 7 350, uses eight cores split evenly between four Zen 5 and four Zen 5c units, which approaches Intel's Core Ultra 9 185H in combined multi-threaded and synthetic benchmark scores, according to early comparisons.
The portable display panel runs at 1920 by 1200 resolution and reportedly covers 100% of the sRGB color gamut.
A 180-degree hinge, 60 Wh battery, and 500 GB NVMe SSD round out the internal hardware package.
Connectivity includes gigabit LAN, Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 5, USB Type-A ports, HDMI output, and a microSD card slot for storage expansion.
A 2 MP webcam with a privacy shutter supports Windows Hello, while Windows 11 Home ships preinstalled on every unit.
Battery life reaches roughly 12.5 hours during video playback and about 25 hours in idle mode, measured under JEITA 3.0 standards.
The Ryzen AI 5 variant, equipped with 16 GB of RAM and 500 GB of storage, costs ¥239,800, or roughly $1,545.
The higher Ryzen AI 7 configuration carries the same memory and storage allotment but costs ¥259,000, or about $1,670.
Sales of this device will begin sequentially from October, though the rollout remains confined to the Japanese market via the company’s online store for now.
The laptop is currently available in three colour options — Cosmo Blue, Frost Gold, and Pearl White.
Both versions ship with single-channel memory by default, though the empty SODIMM slot allows an upgrade path from 16GB to 32 GB.
A compatible 16 GB DDR5-5600 module currently retails for around $229 through Amazon's storefront.
Interested customers elsewhere would need to rely on import services to acquire a unit, adding cost and complexity to the process.

The ongoing bull market, now 2,370 days old, is the longest-running in India, yet it may not feel so for many who entered Dalal Street for the first time in recent years. For years, stock market investors had little patience for fixed-deposits. Equities were the place to be, every dip was an opportunity, and an FD yielding 6-7 per cent looked like financial furniture: safe, familiar and thoroughly unexciting. Turns out, the financial furniture not only held up, it outperformed much of the fancy décor!
For the three-year period ended September 17, 2026, Nifty 50 gained 15.58 per cent and the Sensex just 9.94 per cent on a price-return basis. A three-year SBI fixed deposit available at the start of this period at 6.5 per cent would have generated a cumulative return of about 21.34 per cent. The humble three-year Post Office Time Deposit, then carrying 7 per cent interest, did marginally better at about 21.56 per cent.
The discomfort does not entirely disappear even if the clock is stretched to five years. From September 2021, the Nifty 50 has gained about 32.3 per cent, narrowly ahead of the roughly 30.8 per cent return from a five-year SBI FD, but already below the 34.4 per cent delivered by a five-year Post Office Time Deposit. These last five years represent 77 per cent duration of the current bull market and serves as strong statement to the eccentricities of markets. Even a small correction from current Nifty levels would be enough to push its five-year price return below the SBI FD hurdle rate as well.
For a bull market, losing to an SBI FD and a Post Office deposit is the sort of embarrassment bulls could do without.
The indices are not an isolated embarrassment.
A bl.portfolio analysis of over 2,800 stocks for which comparable adjusted price data were available shows that 1,595, or 56 per cent, failed to beat the three-year Post Office FD return. Almost the same proportion failed to beat SBI FD over the same period.
More strikingly, 1,241 stocks, or about 4 out of 10, actually lost money over these three years.
The median stock gained just 11.25 per cent, roughly half the return generated by the Post Office deposit.
This should not, however, be mistaken for a market where nobody made money. Dalal Street had enough fireworks. As many as 605 stocks, or 21 per cent of the universe, at least doubled. Of these, 289 stocks tripled or more. This highlights how much stock selection, discretion and valuation has mattered as against mindless euphoria.
That is what makes the period unusual. More than two out of five stocks destroyed capital, while one in five became multibaggers. So, Dalal Street, served few investors a bit of pulao while many others were left looking for the papad.
Sector performance shows how selective the rally was. Defence, non-ferrous metals, healthcare and capital goods saw broad participation, while IT, FMCG, cement, logistics and several consumer-facing pockets struggled.
Some of the heavyweight names explain why the headline indices struggled. Among the ten largest companies by market capitalisation in September 2023, only four beat the Post Office hurdle. TCS fell about 39 per cent, ITC 38 per cent, Infosys 29 per cent and Hindustan Unilever 20 per cent. HDFC Bank fell about 12 per cent, while Reliance Industries delivered barely 2 per cent. Apparently, being a bellwethername was no protection against being shown up by the neighbourhood post office.
Against that stood Bharti Airtel, up about 99 per cent, SBI at 63 per cent, ICICI Bank at 36 per cent and Bajaj Finance at 35 per cent.
There is an important nuance here. This was not simply a case of large-caps failing while smaller stocks partied. In fact, the top 100 stocks by September 2023 market capitalisation produced a median return of about 31 per cent, and 58 per cent beat the Post Office.
The odds progressively worsened down the market-cap ladder. Among stocks ranked beyond 1,000, the 3-year median return was only about 8 per cent, just 42 per cent beat the Post Office and nearly 46 per cent lost money.
So the problem was not large-caps generally.
There is another clue in what happened earlier. Between September 2021 and September 2023, the median stock in this universe had gained about 39 per cent and nearly 73 per cent of stocks were positive. In the subsequent three years, the median return shrank to 11.25 per cent and the proportion of gainers fell to about 57 per cent.
Almost 30 per cent of the entire universe consists of stocks that rose during the earlier two-year period but fell over the following three years.
These data points help part explain the strange optics of the current market. Since the March 2020 bottom, the Nifty has seen only four drawdowns exceeding 10 per cent, despite the bull phase stretching for more than six years. But underneath that apparently calm surface, outcomes have become sharply dispersed.
None of this means an FD should replace equities for all investors. But what these numbers do is puncture a more casual assumption: that being invested during a bull market automatically earns investors a handsome premium over boring fixed income. Over the last three years, the boring instruments did their job. A majority of stocks did not! Ignoring the need for margin of safety in equity investments tends to have painful consequences in the long run as the last three-five years have shown.

87,2026-09-19,"by Ivan Johnson, Head of School
Every time a new technology arrives, it finds its way into education, and every time, we are handed the same choice: listen to the people trying to sell it to us, or listen to teachers.
We’ve done this before. In the 1950s and 60s, television was going to be the answer. School districts wired classrooms for broadcast so a single “master teacher” could deliver a lesson to hundreds of classrooms at once. It was pitched exactly the way every new education technology gets pitched: as a way to reach more kids, more consistently, for less money. It didn’t hold up. A kid watching a screen is not the same as a kid in a room with an adult who knows them.
In 2012, Stanford’s then president John Hennessy, gave a keynote called “The Coming Tsunami in Educational Technology.” He predicted that technology was about to hit higher education the way it had already hit newspapers and music, and that colleges needed to drive down their own costs with online learning or get run over by whoever did. He pointed to Khan Academy, and to the first wave of MOOCs (Massive Open Online Courses offered to an unlimited number of learners worldwide) like Coursera and edX as proof it was already underway. He told the room to be the disruptor, not the disrupted.
“The tsunami is coming whether we like it or not,” Coursera founder Daphne Koller famously told The Atlantic. “You can be crushed or you can surf, and it is better to surf.”
That was over a decade ago. MOOCs never replaced universities. Completion rates on them were, and still are, famously terrible. The tsunami mostly reshaped corporate training, not the in-person college experience Hennessy was actually warning about.
Education has a problem the rest of the economy doesn’t. When a car company automates its assembly line, it builds more cars with fewer people, and the cost of the car goes down. A bank that used to need a room full of people to process transactions by hand, now does it in seconds with a computer. Technology makes those industries cheaper, and in some cases better for the end user. But, it has never done that in a classroom. What works in education hasn’t changed: one skilled, present teacher with a small group of kids. While pedagogy and curriculum can and do develop, nothing has ever made the core relationship of a caring adult working closely with a handful of kids cheaper without also making it worse. 
Still, that doesn’t stop people from trying to sell us the idea that it can. Every time a new technology shows up, the companies who stand to profit from it look at education and see a problem they can solve, because solving it would also make them economically successful, in a market worth a great deal of money. It’s really just Upton Sinclair’s old point. He wrote about efficiency being valued over humanity, looking hard at working conditions in the wake of the industrial revolution. He said it’s hard to get someone to see something clearly when their income depends on not seeing it. So the pitch comes fast, and every time, they tell us this one is different. It never is.
I have two stories from my own career where I chose the sales pitch over the people in the room who actually knew better, buying a company’s promise that a piece of technology would just make things better, and finding out that it didn’t. I think we’re about to make that same mistake with AI too.
Smart boards
If you don’t know what a smart board is, that’s because they were dumb. A smart board was a touchscreen panel mounted over a section of a classroom whiteboard, with a projector bolted above it throwing the image down. It ate up about seventy-five percent of your usable whiteboard space, and it worked maybe a quarter of the time.
At my last school, I went to a conference, got sold, and installed them in every classroom, over the objections of teachers who begged me not to take their whiteboard space away. The bill was over fifty thousand dollars. Within two years, almost every teacher in the building had quietly retreated to the small strip of real whiteboard they had left. I eventually took every one of them down and kept the projectors, which is, of course, what everyone eventually used in classrooms anyway: a whiteboard and a projector.
Much later in my career, I watched one of the most gifted middle school Waldorf science teachers draw by freehand in chalk  a cell in more depth and more beauty than I had ever seen in a textbook or on a slide. It made me question whether I needed a projector in the room at all.
Open gradebooks
The second story is harder, because the cost wasn’t financial, but human.
In 2008 when I was 8 years into my teaching career and a middle school advisor, the conversation about open gradebooks first started. Student management systems, the Ravenna or Google Classroom-type platforms, were becoming standard, and the question that came with them was whether to open the gradebook, making every assignment’s grade and every piece of feedback live to students and parents the moment it was entered. It was framed as transparency, and transparency sounds like an obviously good thing.
Shortly after, I became assistant dean and I was part of the team put in charge of rolling out open gradebooks.
The vendors sold it hard. And I remember exactly how the conversation shifted: it was never really a debate about whether we should do it. It became a conversation about when.
Every teacher I knew worried about the same thing. They said kids would start checking their grades constantly, obsessively, that a live grade/number would replace an actual understanding of how students were doing. They said teachers would stop assigning meaningful low-stakes work: early drafts and rough attempts that are supposed to count for little, so a kid can take risks and get real feedback that measures their growth before the pressure of a finished product. Once every point is visible in real time, that growth falls apart. Points become the only thing that matters, and learning stops being about growth and starts being about the running total. They said parents, once they had full-time visibility into a live number, would question grades constantly, and that teachers, already stretched thin, would get worn down by the volume of emails until grades quietly inflated just to keep the peace.
We did not listen to teachers’ concerns. We moved forward with open gradebooks. 
And the teachers’ concerns came true. Grades became inflated and, in turn, meaningless, and I’d argue that’s one of the biggest problems in education right now. Ask any college professor. It’s not just that they’re now fielding emails from parents complaining about their twenty-year-old’s grades, though that happens too. It’s that they’re teaching kids who show up with far more confidence than their actual ability level can back up.
The weight shifted onto tools that felt more objective from the outside: the SAT, the ACT, external standardized measures. That was already the direction college admissions were heading towards. Then Covid hit and paused it, test-optional policies spread fast. But it’s back. I saw it with seniors at my last school, and I see it now with Colorado seniors I work with: straight-A students earning SAT scores well below what straight A’s should predict, confused and frustrated that they’re not getting into the colleges they expected.
Denise Pope, who studies student stress at Stanford’s graduate school of education, has found that when you ask kids what stresses them out most, the answer is almost always the same: grades, tests, and other assessments. And that holds true across rural, urban, public, and private schools alike. I got to work with Denise, about ten years ago, when a friend of mine and I ran our own small version of that research. He was teaching in Boulder, I was in LA, and we pulled data from our own high schoolers on how often they checked their grades. The number came back between a staggering fifty and eighty times a day.
Because grades don’t mean anything anymore, feedback doesn’t either. Imagine if every piece of feedback from your boss was meaningless, and you never actually knew whether you were doing a good job or a bad one. That work environment would be toxic. You’d feel anxious – or apathetic – every single day.
Welcome to education in 2026.
The actual choice
This is the pattern, every time. On one side you have the people who stand to make money from  technology. On the other side you have teachers, who show up every day to do a job I genuinely believe is impossible. You never finish being a teacher. You just get used to trying as hard as you can, every day, for kids who are not yours.
I choose to listen to teachers on the question of technology in schools. In my experience, teachers have never been wrong. And on the issue of AI in schools, they are not divided. Almost every teacher I know is saying some version of the same thing: AI in schools, as it is currently being introduced, is not going to end well for this generation.
Not every piece of technology is useless in a classroom. A photocopier is a tool that makes it easier to prepare for class, which means more time to connect with students and build better curriculum. But it never replaces the teacher deciding what goes on the page. Something like Google Read&Write is the same kind of tool: it gives a kid with dyslexia a way to access the same text as everyone else and get their own ideas onto the page, without changing what the teacher is actually teaching or why. Neither tool replaces anyone. They just make the job a little easier.
AI could be that kind of tool. A teacher who used to spend hours building three or four versions of the same lesson for different learners could do it in minutes now, and spend the saved prep hours back on the kids instead. I’m genuinely open to that.
However, there’s a different version of AI on the table too. The real dividing line isn’t whether a teacher is the one standing up front delivering the lesson. It’s whether AI is expanding what a teacher can do, or replacing the judgment that makes them a teacher in the first place: reading the kid in front of them, deciding what this particular group needs, designing the experience that gets them there, and knowing when to change course. Some of what’s being sold right now does the second thing. It hands AI the decisions about what to teach and how, and asks the adults in the room to become something closer to coaches or monitors. It’s being sold as the future, the way everything is sold as the future. I look at that and think about every other time we believed we had found something that could replace a teacher’s judgment and their relationship with their kids, and every time, kids were the ones who paid for the experiment. I’m comfortable being behind the times while everyone else works out, again, that you cannot replace a teacher.
And this is why education is expensive. That’s the real problem underneath all of it: we have tried, over and over, to make education more efficient, and we can’t, because it never gets cheaper the way everything else does when technology advances. That’s why a school like Friends costs what it costs. It’s also why it’s worth it. Because a child’s education isn’t built on efficiency. It’s built on time, attention, core relationships, and the real people who know them and show up for them. Every single day.
Friends School"

98,2026-09-19,"Annuity or invested portfolio? It's one of the most debated questions in retirement planning. Both sides have real arguments, which is exactly why we asked ChatGPT to break them down honestly.
What it found: the answer isn't about picking a winner. It's about understanding the core tension between guaranteed income and portfolio flexibility — and more importantly, which trade-off actually matters for your situation.
Keep Learning: I Asked ChatGPT How Much To Keep in Cash in Retirement — Here's What It Said
Learn How: 5 Clever Ways Retirees Are Earning Up to $1K Per Month From Home
An annuity converts a lump sum into guaranteed lifetime income, shifting market risk entirely to the insurance company. A liquid invested portfolio keeps the capital growing, accessible and available to pass to heirs, but the retiree absorbs all the market fluctuation.
Neither is universally smarter. The right answer depends on how much of your essential monthly spending is already covered by guaranteed income sources.
ChatGPT's case for an annuity centers on what it called the income floor gap. First, add up your monthly income and expenses. If guaranteed income doesn't fully cover those expenses, a market downturn can directly disrupt the basics of daily life.
An annuity, typically a Single Premium Immediate Annuity or a Fixed Index Annuity with a Guaranteed Lifetime Withdrawal Benefit, fills that gap with pension-like reliability. The insurer absorbs the market risk. The retiree gets a predictable check regardless of what the stock market does that quarter.
Beyond the math, ChatGPT flagged the behavioral argument that retirees with guaranteed income streams tend to spend more comfortably and carry less financial anxiety than those relying entirely on a portfolio. If market volatility causes a retiree to panic and sell when prices are down, transferring that risk to an insurance company protects against that.
If Social Security and pension income already cover fixed monthly expenses, the portfolio only needs to fund discretionary spending. In that case, locking capital into an annuity sacrifices liquidity and legacy for income certainty that isn't actually needed.
A liquid diversified portfolio — something in the range of 60% equities and 40% bonds and cash — outperforms an annuity on several dimensions when the income floor is already covered. It leaves 100% of remaining assets to heirs rather than reverting to the insurer at death. It stays accessible for large, unexpected expenses like medical events or home repairs. And over a 20- to 30-year retirement, equities provide dividend growth and capital appreciation that fixed annuity payments — which typically don't adjust for inflation — simply cannot match.
ChatGPT's most useful framing was the hybrid, which it described as a floor and upside structure. Essential expenses get covered by guaranteed income — Social Security, any pension and if needed, a fixed annuity sized precisely to fill the remaining gap. Discretionary spending gets funded by a flexible invested portfolio positioned for growth, inflation defense and legacy.
The annuity covers only the exact monthly amount needed to bridge the essential spending gap and not a dollar more. Everything else stays liquid. This approach buys lifetime peace of mind for the non-negotiable expenses while leaving the majority of the nest egg positioned to outpace inflation, handle emergencies and transfer to heirs.
